Moreover, the projected pairs $(b_{\alpha,x}/L,L\xi_\alpha)$ have bounded counts in unit balls for $\alpha\in\mathcal A_s$. Indeed, over any such ball, the remaining coordinates $(b_{\alpha,y}/L,L\eta_\alpha)$ lie in a bounded box by <a href="#eq:flat-normal-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-normal-width">[eq:flat-normal-width]</a> and <a href="#eq:flat-normal-intercepts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-normal-intercepts">[eq:flat-normal-intercepts]</a>. Its center may depend on $s$, but it is covered by a fixed number of unit balls. The original four-dimensional phase-space bound therefore bounds the number of projected labels uniformly. Projection does not cost a power of $L$.

Let $\nu_s$ be the pushforward to $(x,t)$ of the restriction of the transformed measure to slab $s$. For any bounded tangential velocity $v$ and $1\leq\rho\leq r\leq L^2$, the inverse image of a test in <a href="#eq:flat-tube-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-tube-test">[eq:flat-tube-test]</a> has dimensions comparable to $r$, $\rho$, and $L$, after a shear of bounded norm. It can be covered by $$C\frac r\rho\left(1+\frac L\rho\right)$$ balls of radius $C\rho$. These radii are at least one. Applying the ball bound gives <a id="eq:flat-projected-test"></a>$$\label{eq:flat-projected-test}
 \nu_s\{|t-t_0|\leq r,\ |x-b-2vt|\leq\rho\}
      \leq Cmr(L+\rho)
      =CmL\,r\left(1+\frac\rho L\right).$$ Thus Proposition <a href="#prop:oned" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:oned">36</a> applies to $\nu_s$ with $m_1=CmL$ and any fixed tangential frequency interval containing the labels.

<a id="eq:flat-mode-decay"></a>
It remains to handle the dependence of the field on the normal coordinate. A pointwise choice of a normal slice would not suffice for an arbitrary measure, so we use a single Fourier expansion valid throughout the slab. Put $$z=\frac{x-b_{\alpha,x}-2\xi_\alpha t}{L},\qquad
 \tau=\frac{t}{L^2},\qquad w=\frac{y-y_s}{L},\qquad
 \zeta_{\alpha,s}=\frac{y_s-b_{\alpha,y}}L,
 \quad a_\alpha=L\eta_\alpha.$$ Choose a fixed smooth cutoff $\chi(w)$ equal to one for $|w|\leq1/2$ and supported strictly inside one period of fixed length $T$. Expand <a id="eq:flat-normal-series"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 F_{\alpha,s}(z,\tau,w)
   &:=\chi(w)e^{i a_\alpha w-i a_\alpha^2\tau}
       H_\alpha(z,w+\zeta_{\alpha,s}-2a_\alpha\tau,\tau)
       \notag\\
   &=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}e^{2\pi i n w/T}
                          F_{\alpha,s,n}(z,\tau).
       \label{eq:flat-normal-series}
\end{aligned}$$ The constants $a_\alpha$ and $\zeta_{\alpha,s}$ are bounded on $\mathcal A_s$. Consequently, if $D$ is the finite derivative order needed in Proposition <a href="#prop:oned" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:oned">36</a>, integration by parts in $w$ gives, for any sufficiently large fixed $N$ for which the original profiles have $D+N$ derivatives, $$\label{eq:flat-mode-decay}
 \max_{j+k\leq D}
  \|\partial_z^j\partial_\tau^kF_{\alpha,s,n}\|_\infty
       \leq C_N(1+|n|)^{-N}.$$ All these profiles have support in a common bounded $(z,\tau)$ box. The residual normal phase has bounded derivatives in these scaled variables; this is the reason for first removing the central normal frequency. The remaining constant phase $e^{iy_s\eta_\alpha}$ can be included in the coefficient.

<a id="eq:flat-strip-series"></a>
Absorb $C_N(1+|n|)^{-N}$ from <a href="#eq:flat-mode-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-mode-decay">[eq:flat-mode-decay]</a> into the coefficients and normalize each remaining profile. This produces one-dimensional fields $V_{s,n}$ with uniform admissible profiles and the already verified projected phase-space counts, such that in slab $s$ $$\label{eq:flat-strip-series}
 U(x,y,t)=L^{-1/2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}
       e^{2\pi i n (y-y_s)/(TL)}V_{s,n}(x,t),
 \qquad
 \|c_{s,n}\|_2\leq C_N(1+|n|)^{-N}e_s,$$ where $e_s^2=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal A_s}|c_\alpha|^2$. The series converges absolutely, and all its external phase factors have modulus one at every witness. The factor $L^{-1/2}$ outside the series is the difference between the two-dimensional packet normalization $L^{-1}$ and the one-dimensional normalization $L^{-1/2}$.

<a id="eq:flat-mode-sum"></a>
Choose positive numbers $a_n=c_*(1+|n|)^{-2}$ with $\sum_n a_n=1/2$. If $|U(x,y,t)|\geq gL^{-4/3}$ in slab $s$, then <a href="#eq:flat-strip-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-strip-series">[eq:flat-strip-series]</a> forces, for at least one $n$, $$|V_{s,n}(x,t)|\geq a_ngL^{-5/6}.$$ Taking the projected measure of this union and applying Proposition <a href="#prop:oned" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:oned">36</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mu\bigl(\{|U|\geq gL^{-4/3}\}\cap\text{slab }s\bigr)
 &\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}
       \nu_s\{|V_{s,n}|\geq a_ngL^{-5/6}\}
       \notag\\
 &\leq C mL^4g^{-p_0}e_s^{p_0}
       \sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}a_n^{-p_0}(1+|n|)^{-Np_0}
       \notag\\
 &\leq C mL^4g^{-p_0}e_s^{p_0},
       \label{eq:flat-mode-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ provided $(N-2)p_0>1$. Only finitely many derivative bounds are needed to choose such an $N$. Finally, $$\sum_s e_s^2\leq C\|c\|_2^2,
 \qquad
 \sum_s e_s^{p_0}
       \leq\left(\sum_s e_s^2\right)^{p_0/2}
       \leq C\|c\|_2^{p_0},$$ by bounded slab overlap and $p_0>2$. Summing <a href="#eq:flat-mode-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-mode-sum">[eq:flat-mode-sum]</a> proves <a href="#eq:src10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src10">[eq:src10]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-flat-time"></a>
<div id="rem:flat-time" class="remark">

*Remark 38* (Fixed enlargements of the time domain). The preceding conclusions also hold on $|t|\leq C_tL^2$ for any fixed $C_t$, with constants depending on $C_t$. Partition this range into boundedly many intervals of radius at most $L^2$, and translate the center of each interval to zero. A time translation by $t_*$ replaces the packet intercept $b$ by $b+2\omega t_*$ and shifts the profile time argument by $t_*/L^2$; fixed support and derivative bounds remain uniform. On phase space this is the bounded shear $(b/L,L\omega)\mapsto(b/L+2(t_*/L^2)L\omega,L\omega)$, so real-center ball counts remain bounded. The one-dimensional tube hypothesis is invariant under this translation because its time centers and intercepts are arbitrary; the two-dimensional ball hypothesis is translation invariant as well. Restriction to each time interval and a bounded sum therefore give the asserted extension.

</div>

<a id="sec:geometry"></a>
### Rich tubes lie in a short list of plates

We next isolate a geometric consequence of two simultaneous properties: every tube sees substantial mass, and the tubes available at each point have a spread of directions. Both properties must concern the same measure. The conclusion is a short list of planes near which all the tube trajectories lie. The first part of the proof finds a rich plane through each target line; the second part packs these planes, keeping track of their offsets as well as their normals.

Divide all three physical coordinates by $L$, put $H=L$, and divide the original measure by $mL^2$. In these coordinates the ball hypothesis becomes $\nu(B(q,r))\lesssim r^2$ for $r\geq1$, the relevant domain has diameter $O(H)$, and the tubes have unit width. This section uses these coordinates throughout.

<a id="stmt-prop-rich"></a>
<div id="prop:rich" class="proposition">

**Proposition 39** (Rich-tube geometry). *Fix bounded velocity, domain, ball, and neighborhood constants, a richness constant $c_{\mathrm r}>0$, an angular constant $C_{\mathrm a}>0$, exponents $A_{\mathrm r},A_{\mathrm a}\geq0$, and $\eta>0$. There are constants $A_*,C_*,C'$ depending only on these fixed data such that the following holds whenever $X\geq2$ and $H\geq C_*X^{A_*}$.*

*Let $\nu$ be a nonnegative measure supported in a ball of radius $O(H)$, with $$\nu(B(q,r))\leq C_{\mathrm b}r^2\qquad(r\geq1).$$ Let $\mathcal T$ be a finite family of lines $$\ell_T=\{(b_T+2\omega_Tt,t):t\in\mathbb R\},\qquad
 |\omega_T|\leq C_{\mathrm v},$$ viewed as unit-width tubes over a fixed time interval of length $O(H)$ containing the time projection of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu$. Write $d_T(x,t)=|x-b_T-2\omega_Tt|$ and $e_T=(2\omega_T,1)/|(2\omega_T,1)|$. Assume:*

<a id="eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis"></a>
1.  *Every tube is rich against $\nu$: $$\label{eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis}
     \int (1+d_T(q))^{-M}\,d\nu(q)
     \geq c_{\mathrm r} H X^{-A_{\mathrm r}},\qquad M=100.$$*

<a id="eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis"></a>
2.  *At $\nu$-almost every $q$ there is a measurably chosen probability $(p_T(q))_{T\in\mathcal T}$, supported on tubes with $d_T(q)\leq C_{\mathrm n}$, such that every spherical direction ball satisfies $$\label{eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis}
     \sum_{e_T\in B_{\mathbb S^2}(e,r)}p_T(q)
     \leq C_{\mathrm a}X^{A_{\mathrm a}}r^\eta
     \qquad(r\geq H^{-1}).$$*

<a id="eq:src11"></a>
*Then each tube can be assigned to one of at most $X^{C'}$ plates (planes thickened by $X^{C'}$). Its assigned plate contains its central line segment over the relevant time interval. If $n$ is a unit normal to that plate, then $$\label{eq:src11}
 |n\cdot(2\omega_T,1)|\leq X^{C'}/H.$$ The constants are independent of the number of tubes. In particular, the richness in <a href="#eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis">[eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis]</a> and the pointwise law in <a href="#eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis">[eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis]</a> use exactly the same measure $\nu$.*

</div>

Fixed factors may be absorbed into fixed powers of $X$, since $X\geq2$. We write $X^{O(1)}$ only for powers depending on the data in the proposition; the order in which all additional exponents are chosen is specified at the end of the proof. No estimate below uses a ball bound at radii smaller than one. Bounded velocities also give $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(q,\ell_T)\leq d_T(q)\leq C\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(q,\ell_T)$, so transverse deviation and orthogonal distance define comparable neighborhoods. For a finite family, measurability of the neighboring law adds no restriction: the set $\{T:d_T(q)\leq C_{\mathrm n}\}$ takes only finitely many values, and one may choose an admissible probability once for each occurring incidence pattern. These patterns are Borel, since each $d_T$ is continuous, so the resulting law is measurable.

<a id="stmt-lem-geometry-energy"></a>
<div id="lem:geometry-energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:geometry-parameter-energy"></a>
**Lemma 40** (A truncated parameter energy). *Let $H\geq1$ and let $\sigma$ be a finite measure on $\mathbb R^2$ of mass $m$, satisfying $$\sigma(B(z,r))\leq A r^{1+\eta}
 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^2,\ r\geq H^{-1}),\qquad A>0.$$ Set $\gamma=\eta/(1+\eta)$. Then $$\label{eq:geometry-parameter-energy}
 \iint \min\{H,|z-z'|^{-1}\}\,d\sigma(z')\,d\sigma(z)
 \leq C_\eta A^{1/(1+\eta)}m^{1+\gamma},$$ where the kernel on the diagonal is $H$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-geometry-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-geometry-energy</summary>

*Proof.* The assertion is immediate when $m=0$. Otherwise put $R=(m/A)^{1/(1+\eta)}$. If $R<H^{-1}$, the inner integral is at most $$Hm\leq m/R=A^{1/(1+\eta)}m^\gamma.$$ If $R\geq H^{-1}$, the contribution of $|z-z'|\leq H^{-1}$ is at most $AH^{-\eta}$. The dyadic annuli between $H^{-1}$ and $R$ contribute at most $$C\sum_{H^{-1}\leq 2^jH^{-1}\lesssim R}
       A(2^jH^{-1})^\eta
 \leq C_\eta AR^\eta.$$ The remaining region contributes at most $m/R$. Thus the inner integral is bounded by $C_\eta A^{1/(1+\eta)}m^\gamma$ in both cases. Integrating in $z$ proves <a href="#eq:geometry-parameter-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-parameter-energy">[eq:geometry-parameter-energy]</a>. The cutoff at $H$ is what permits the first case without a hypothesis below $H^{-1}$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-geometry-energy-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-geometry-energy</summary>

<a id="eq:geometry-local-richness"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:rich" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rich">39</a>.* *1. Localize richness and choose a longitudinal point.* For $1\leq w\leq H$, a width-$w$ neighborhood of any relevant trajectory can be covered by $O(H/w)$ balls of radius $O(w)$. Consequently <a id="eq:geometry-tube-cover"></a>$$\label{eq:geometry-tube-cover}
 \nu\{d_T\leq w\}\lesssim Hw.$$ For $w\geq H$ the same upper bound follows from $\nu(\mathbb R^3)\lesssim H^2$. Therefore the part of the smoothed integral outside width $W\geq1$ is at most $$C\sum_{j\geq0}(2^jW)^{-M}H(2^{j+1}W)
 \lesssim HW^{1-M}.$$ Choose $W=X^{c_1}$, with $(M-1)c_1>A_{\mathrm r}$ by a sufficiently large fixed margin. It follows from <a href="#eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis">[eq:geometry-rich-hypothesis]</a> that, for every $T$, $$\label{eq:geometry-local-richness}
 \nu\{d_T\leq W\}\geq cH X^{-A_{\mathrm r}}.$$ We always take $H$ large enough that $W\ll H$.

Fix a target tube $T_0$, and abbreviate its line and unit direction by $\ell$ and $e$. Translate the origin to a point of $\ell$ at distance $O(W)$ from the original domain. Such a point exists by <a href="#eq:geometry-local-richness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-local-richness">[eq:geometry-local-richness]</a>. The measure domain, and all relevant trajectory segments, remain inside a ball of radius $O(H)$: the latter assertion follows for every tube from its own localized richness, bounded velocity, and the common $O(H)$ time range.

<a id="eq:geometry-longitudinal"></a>
Draw $q_0$ according to normalized $\nu$ restricted to $\{d_{T_0}\leq W\}$, and let $s=H^{-1}e\cdot q_0$. Thus $Hs$ is the longitudinal coordinate of the orthogonal projection of $q_0$ onto $\ell$. A longitudinal window of relative length $r\geq H^{-1}$ inside this width-$O(W)$ tube is covered by $O(1+Hr/W)$ balls of radius $O(W)$. Before normalization its mass is at most $C(W^2+HrW)$; hence $$\label{eq:geometry-longitudinal}
 \mathbb P\{s\in I\}\lesssim
 X^{A_{\mathrm r}}(W^2/H+rW)
 \leq X^{O(1)}r,
 \qquad |I|\leq r,\quad r\geq H^{-1}.$$ In the last inequality we used precisely $H^{-1}\leq r$.

*2. Sample a transverse neighbor and keep bin measures unnormalized.* Choose $\delta=X^{-c_0}$, with $c_0$ large relative to $A_{\mathrm a}/\eta$. At every sampled point discard directions with $|e\times e_T|<\delta$. They lie in the union of two spherical balls about $e$ and $-e$ of radius $O(\delta)$, so <a href="#eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis">[eq:geometry-angular-hypothesis]</a> makes their total probability less than $1/2$. Here $H^{-1}\leq\delta$ is included in the scale hierarchy. Renormalize the remaining probabilities separately at each $q_0$. This costs at most two in the angular bound and does not change the marginal law of $q_0$ or <a href="#eq:geometry-longitudinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-longitudinal">[eq:geometry-longitudinal]</a>. Draw a tube $T$ from this remaining law. It is a physical neighbor of $q_0$, and is rich against the original common measure $\nu$.

Partition azimuth about the target axis $e$ into half-open intervals of length comparable to $H^{-1}$. For a bin $\pi$, let $u_\pi$ be its central radial unit vector in $e^\perp$, and let $P_\pi$ be the exact plane through $\ell$ spanned by $e,u_\pi$. For a sampled unit direction $e'$ in this bin define $$a=\frac{e\cdot e'}{u_\pi\cdot e'}.$$ The denominator is at least $c\delta$, so $|a|\lesssim\delta^{-1}$. Let $\sigma_\pi$ be the pushforward to $(s,a)$ of the sampling probability *restricted* to this azimuth bin, and write $m_\pi=\|\sigma_\pi\|$. We have <a id="eq:geometry-bin-masses"></a>$$\label{eq:geometry-bin-masses}
 \sum_\pi m_\pi=1.$$ In particular, we have not divided $\sigma_\pi$ by $m_\pi$.

<a id="eq:geometry-joint-frostman"></a>
For completeness, the angular estimate remains effective in these coordinates without conditioning on the bin. If $n_\pi$ completes $e,u_\pi$ to an orthonormal basis and $\theta$ is azimuth relative to $u_\pi$, then $$e'=\frac{ae+u_\pi+\tan\theta\,n_\pi}
             {(1+a^2+\tan^2\theta)^{1/2}},
 \qquad |\theta|\lesssim H^{-1}.$$ An interval of length $O(r)$ in $a$, within this bin, is therefore contained in a direction ball of radius $O(r)$ when $r\geq H^{-1}$; allowing $X^{O(1)}r$ would also suffice. For a ball in the $(s,a)$-plane of radius $r$, integrate the conditional angular bound over the corresponding longitudinal event in <a href="#eq:geometry-longitudinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-longitudinal">[eq:geometry-longitudinal]</a>. This gives $$\label{eq:geometry-joint-frostman}
 \sigma_\pi(B(z,r))\leq X^{A_{\mathrm F}}r^{1+\eta}
 \qquad(r\geq H^{-1})$$ for any $A_{\mathrm F}>A_{\mathrm r}+2c_1+A_{\mathrm a}$ with a sufficiently large fixed margin. This is a bound for the unnormalized measure. No assertion about angular nonconcentration conditional on landing in a small bin is needed.

*3. Strips in the bin plane.* Write $x=e\cdot q$ and $y=u_\pi\cdot q$. Choose $w=X^{c_2}$ with $c_2>c_0+c_1$ by a sufficiently large fixed margin, and define the plate and its strip multiplicity by <a id="eq:geometry-strip-multiplicity"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 Q_\pi&=\{q\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(q,P_\pi)\leq w\},\notag\\
 F_\pi(q)&=\int
  \mathbf1_{\{|x-Hs-ay|\leq w\}}\,d\sigma_\pi(s,a).
 \label{eq:geometry-strip-multiplicity}
\end{aligned}$$ For each sampled pair $(q_0,T)$ in bin $\pi$, the whole width-$W$ rich portion of $T$ lies in $Q_\pi$ and in its associated strip. Indeed, write $q=q_0+\tau e_T+\varepsilon$ with $|\varepsilon|\lesssim W$ and $|\tau|\lesssim H$. Azimuth binning gives $|n_\pi\cdot e_T|\lesssim H^{-1}$, while $e\cdot e_T-a u_\pi\cdot e_T=0$. Since the displacement of $q_0$ from its projection $Hs e$ is $O(W)$, the plate error is $O(W+1)$ and the strip error is $O((1+|a|)W)$, both bounded by $w$.

<a id="eq:geometry-strip-intersection"></a>
<a id="eq:geometry-strip-kernel"></a>
Consider two strips with parameters $z=(s,a)$ and $z'=(s',a')$. At an intersection point, $$\label{eq:geometry-strip-intersection}
 |H(s-s')+(a-a')y|\leq2w,\qquad |y|\lesssim H.$$ In particular, $$|s-s'|\leq C|a-a'|+2w/H.$$ Put $d=|z-z'|$. If $d\leq Cw/H$, covering the full strip by unit balls costs $X^{O(1)}H$, which is at most $X^{O(1)}\min\{H,d^{-1}\}$ after increasing the fixed power. If $d>Cw/H$, with $C$ chosen from the domain bound, the existence of an intersection in <a href="#eq:geometry-strip-intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-strip-intersection">[eq:geometry-strip-intersection]</a> forces $|a-a'|\gtrsim d$. The allowed $y$-interval then has length $O(w/d)$. Its cross-section, the plate thickness, and the factor $1+|a|$ cost only fixed powers of $X$ in a unit-ball covering. The extra constant in the count of unit intervals is also absorbed, because $d\leq X^{O(1)}$. If the offset difference dominates so strongly that <a href="#eq:geometry-strip-intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-strip-intersection">[eq:geometry-strip-intersection]</a> cannot hold, the intersection is empty. Thus in all cases the intersection has a covering by $$\label{eq:geometry-strip-kernel}
 X^{A_{\mathrm I}}\min\{H,|z-z'|^{-1}\}
 \quad\hbox{unit balls}$$ For example, the covering argument gives $Cw^3(1+\delta^{-1})\min\{H,d^{-1}\}$, so any $A_{\mathrm I}>3c_2+c_0$ with a sufficiently large fixed margin suffices in <a href="#eq:geometry-strip-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-strip-kernel">[eq:geometry-strip-kernel]</a>.

<a id="eq:geometry-strip-energy"></a>
Use the ball bound at radius one, Fubini’s theorem, and Lemma <a href="#lem:geometry-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:geometry-energy">40</a> with <a href="#eq:geometry-joint-frostman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-joint-frostman">[eq:geometry-joint-frostman]</a>. For $\gamma=\eta/(1+\eta)$ this yields $$\label{eq:geometry-strip-energy}
 \int_{Q_\pi}F_\pi^2\,d\nu
 \leq X^{A_{\mathrm E}}m_\pi^{1+\gamma}.$$ One can take any sufficiently enlarged exponent $A_{\mathrm E}\geq A_{\mathrm I}+A_{\mathrm F}/(1+\eta)$. There is no remaining factor of $H$ in this estimate.

*4. Richness away from the target and overlap of the far plates.* Put $R_{\mathrm f}=H/X^{c_3}$, choosing $c_3$ after $c_0,c_1,c_2$, and let $$Q_\pi^{\mathrm{far}}
   =Q_\pi\cap\{q:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(q,\ell)\geq R_{\mathrm f}\}.$$ The geometric meaning of the strip and this exclusion is shown in Figure <a href="#fig:geometry-far" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:geometry-far">2</a>.

<figure id="fig:geometry-far">

<figcaption>Geometry in the bin plane $P_{π}$ (schematic projection). The shaded horizontal band is the near region removed in <a href="#eq:geometry-near-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-near-mass">[eq:geometry-near-mass]</a>; the thin sloping strip contains the sampled tube after enlargement. Since its direction is separated from the target, only a controlled length lies in the near band. The true plate also has thickness $w$ perpendicular to this picture.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-geometry-far"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:geometry-far`)

 \draw[densely dashed,black!45] (-0.5,0.7)--(6.2,0.7);
 \draw[densely dashed,black!45] (-0.5,-0.7)--(6.2,-0.7);
 \draw[->] (-0.5,0)--(6.5,0) node[right] {$e$};
 \draw[->] (0,-1.25)--(0,2.85) node[above] {$u_\pi$};
 \node[below] at (5.15,0) {target line $\ell$};
 \fill[blue!9] (0.5,-1.1)--(0.95,-1.1)--(4.7,2.5)--(4.25,2.5)--cycle;
 \draw[blue!65!black,thick,->] (0.73,-1.1)--(4.48,2.5)
       node[above right] {sampled direction $e'$};
 \draw[blue!50!black,densely dashed] (0.5,-1.1)--(4.25,2.5);

       node[midway,right] {$R_{\mathrm f}$};

- A vertical axis on the left labeled $u_\pi$ with an upward arrow, with a point marked $P_\pi$.
- A horizontal line labeled "target line $\ell$" with an arrow pointing right labeled $e$.
- A horizontal shaded band surrounding the target line $\ell$, with its vertical half-height marked by a vertical double-headed arrow labeled $R_f$.
- A thin sloping strip crossing the horizontal band at an angle, labeled below as "strip: $|x - Hs - ay| \le w$".
- An arrow along the upper portion of the strip pointing in the direction of the strip labeled "sampled direction $e'$".
- The portion of the strip inside the shaded band is labeled $Hs$.
- The portion of the sloping strip above the near band contains hatching and is labeled with an arrow pointing to "retained far mass".

<a id="eq:geometry-near-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:geometry-strip-first-moment"></a>
Because a sampled tube makes sine-angle at least $\delta$ with $\ell$, the length of its width-$W$ portion within distance $R_{\mathrm f}$ of $\ell$ is at most $C\delta^{-1}(W+R_{\mathrm f})$. Covering it by radius-$O(W)$ balls gives the mass bound $$\label{eq:geometry-near-mass}
 C\delta^{-1}W(W+R_{\mathrm f})
 \leq C X^{c_0+2c_1}
       +CHX^{c_0+c_1-c_3}.$$ Choose $c_3>A_{\mathrm r}+c_0+c_1$ by a large fixed margin, and then require $H\gg X^{A_{\mathrm r}+c_0+2c_1}$. The bound in <a href="#eq:geometry-near-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-near-mass">[eq:geometry-near-mass]</a> is less than half the lower bound in <a href="#eq:geometry-local-richness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-local-richness">[eq:geometry-local-richness]</a>, uniformly for every sampled tube. Integrating their retained richness against the sampling law therefore gives $$\label{eq:geometry-strip-first-moment}
 \int_{Q_\pi^{\mathrm{far}}}F_\pi\,d\nu
 \geq cHX^{-A_{\mathrm r}}m_\pi.$$ This step uses the richness of every neighboring tube against $\nu$, including mass far from the point at which it was sampled.

<a id="eq:geometry-far-plate-mass"></a>
Cauchy–Schwarz, <a href="#eq:geometry-strip-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-strip-energy">[eq:geometry-strip-energy]</a>, and <a href="#eq:geometry-strip-first-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-strip-first-moment">[eq:geometry-strip-first-moment]</a> imply $$\label{eq:geometry-far-plate-mass}
 \nu(Q_\pi^{\mathrm{far}})
 \geq H^2X^{-K_0}m_\pi^{1-\gamma}$$ for some $K_0\geq2A_{\mathrm r}+A_{\mathrm E}$, enlarged to absorb fixed constants.

<a id="eq:geometry-rich-plane"></a>
All planes $P_\pi$ pass through the same line $\ell$. At a point $q$ at distance $\rho\geq R_{\mathrm f}$ from that line, the condition $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(q,P_\pi)\leq w$ restricts $u_\pi$ to two azimuth arcs of length $O(w/\rho)$. The two arcs account for opposite radial directions. Since bins have size comparable to $H^{-1}$, their overlap multiplicity is at most $$C(1+Hw/R_{\mathrm f})\leq X^{A_{\mathrm O}},
 \qquad A_{\mathrm O}>c_2+c_3$$ after a fixed enlargement. Take $H\gg X^{c_2+c_3}$ so that $w\ll R_{\mathrm f}$. Summing <a href="#eq:geometry-far-plate-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-far-plate-mass">[eq:geometry-far-plate-mass]</a>, using this overlap and $\nu(\mathbb R^3)\lesssim H^2$, gives <a id="eq:geometry-bin-concentration"></a>$$\label{eq:geometry-bin-concentration}
 \sum_\pi m_\pi^{1-\gamma}\leq X^{K_1}$$ for a fixed $K_1>K_0+A_{\mathrm O}$. But $$1=\sum_\pi m_\pi
 \leq (\max_\pi m_\pi)^\gamma
          \sum_\pi m_\pi^{1-\gamma}.$$ Hence some bin has $m_\pi\geq X^{-K_1/\gamma}$. Its plane contains the exact target line, and its width-$w$ plate has mass at least $$\label{eq:geometry-rich-plane}
 H^2X^{-K},\qquad
 K\geq K_0+(1-\gamma)K_1/\gamma.$$ We have produced such a rich plane for every target tube.

*5. Pack rich planes using both normal and offset separation.* Return to a common origin at the center of the original domain. Write each plane just obtained as $$P_j=\{q:n_j\cdot q=b_j\},\qquad |n_j|=1,$$ with $(n_j,b_j)$ identified with $(-n_j,-b_j)$. The width-$w$ plates, restricted to $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu$, all have mass at least $\lambda H^2$, where $\lambda=X^{-K}$. Let $C_{\mathrm t}$ be a fixed bound for $\nu(\mathbb R^3)/H^2$.

<a id="eq:geometry-plate-intersection"></a>
<a id="eq:geometry-offset-threshold"></a>
Choose an angular threshold $\vartheta=X^{c_4}/H$, with $c_4>2c_2+2K$ by a sufficiently large fixed margin. If the projective angle between two normals is at least $\vartheta$, the intersection of their width-$w$ plates inside the domain is contained in a box with dimensions $$O(H)\ \times\ O(w)\ \times\
 O\bigl(w/|\sin\angle(n_i,n_j)|\bigr).$$ Here projective angles lie in $[0,\pi/2]$, and $\vartheta\ll1$. Unit-ball covering thus gives $$\label{eq:geometry-plate-intersection}
 \nu(Q_i\cap Q_j)
 \leq C H^2X^{2c_2-c_4}
 \leq \varepsilon H^2,
 \qquad \varepsilon\leq\lambda^2/(2C_{\mathrm t}).$$ For a pair of normals at projective angle below $\vartheta$, align their signs. Changing the normal then changes its defining linear function by at most $C H\vartheta$ on the domain. Choose an offset threshold $$\label{eq:geometry-offset-threshold}
 \Delta=X^{c_5}>2w+CH\vartheta,
 \qquad c_5>\max\{c_2,c_4\}$$ with sufficiently large fixed margins. Such a close-normal pair with $|b_i-b_j|\geq\Delta$ has disjoint plates on the domain.

Select a maximal sublist in which every pair is separated either by projective normal angle at least $\vartheta$, or, after aligning closer normals, by offset at least $\Delta$. Finiteness makes this selection immediate. If the list has length $N$, set $G=\sum_{j=1}^N\mathbf1_{Q_j}$. Its first and second moments obey $$\int G\,d\nu\geq N\lambda H^2,
 \qquad
 \int G^2\,d\nu\leq C_{\mathrm t}NH^2+\varepsilon N^2H^2.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and the total mass bound give $$N^2\lambda^2
 \leq C_{\mathrm t}^2N+C_{\mathrm t}\varepsilon N^2.$$ By the choice in <a href="#eq:geometry-plate-intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-plate-intersection">[eq:geometry-plate-intersection]</a>, the last term is absorbed, and <a id="eq:geometry-plate-count"></a>$$\label{eq:geometry-plate-count}
 N\leq2C_{\mathrm t}^2\lambda^{-2}\leq X^{O(1)}.$$

Maximality says that each original plane is close to a selected one in both its projective normal and its aligned offset. On the entire bounded range of relevant trajectory segments, this means that its central line lies within $\Delta+CH\vartheta=X^{O(1)}$ of the selected plane. Enlarge the selected plates accordingly. If $n_0$ is the original normal of the plane through the target line and $n$ is the aligned selected normal, then $$n_0\cdot(2\omega_T,1)=0,\qquad |n-n_0|\lesssim\vartheta.$$ Bounded velocity now gives $|n\cdot(2\omega_T,1)|\lesssim X^{c_4}/H$, proving <a href="#eq:src11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src11">[eq:src11]</a>. Offset control is essential for containment of the segment; angular control supplies the asserted directional bound.

*Constant hierarchy.* Here is one complete order of choices. First fix the data in the proposition and $M=100$. Choose $c_0,c_1$ so that $$c_0\eta>A_{\mathrm a},\qquad
 (M-1)c_1>A_{\mathrm r},$$ with fixed margins that give the two required half-mass losses. Next choose $c_2>c_0+c_1$, followed by $$A_{\mathrm F}>A_{\mathrm r}+2c_1+A_{\mathrm a},\qquad
 A_{\mathrm I}>3c_2+c_0,\qquad
 A_{\mathrm E}>A_{\mathrm I}+A_{\mathrm F}/(1+\eta),$$ with sufficient fixed margins for <a href="#eq:geometry-joint-frostman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-joint-frostman">[eq:geometry-joint-frostman]</a>–<a href="#eq:geometry-strip-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-strip-energy">[eq:geometry-strip-energy]</a>. Choose $$c_3>\max\{c_2,A_{\mathrm r}+c_0+c_1\},$$ and then choose $A_{\mathrm O},K_0,K_1,K$ as in <a href="#eq:geometry-far-plate-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-far-plate-mass">[eq:geometry-far-plate-mass]</a>–<a href="#eq:geometry-rich-plane" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-rich-plane">[eq:geometry-rich-plane]</a>. Choose $c_4>2c_2+2K$ to ensure <a href="#eq:geometry-plate-intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-plate-intersection">[eq:geometry-plate-intersection]</a>, and then $c_5>\max\{c_2,c_4\}$ to ensure <a href="#eq:geometry-offset-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:geometry-offset-threshold">[eq:geometry-offset-threshold]</a>. Choose $C'$ larger than $c_5,c_4,2K$, with a fixed margin absorbing the final widths, counts, and direction constants. Finally take $$A_*>\max\{A_{\mathrm r}+c_0+2c_1,\ c_2+c_3,\ c_0,\ c_4,\ C'\},$$ and $C_*$ sufficiently large. Every strict inequality here includes an additional fixed margin if necessary for the fixed constants. All choices precede $X,H$; in particular no exponent depends on the number of tubes or on an azimuth-bin mass. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:dyadic"></a>
### The dyadic distribution estimate

The sparse estimate supplies a gain when every packet meets little measure. For a general array, we first remove sparse packets and narrow angular contributions. The remaining packets lie in a controlled collection of plates. The main analytic step then replaces all intermediate packet scales by a single family at a shorter scale, to which the bilinear frame estimate applies.

<a id="stmt-thm-distribution"></a>
<div id="thm:distribution" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:src12"></a>
**Theorem 41** (Dyadic distribution bound). *There are fixed $p>2$ and $\sigma>0$ with the following property. Let $U$ be a finite sum of the atoms in <a href="#eq:src5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src5">[eq:src5]</a>, of packet size $L\geq1$, with $\sum_\alpha|c_\alpha|^2\leq1$. Suppose that $\mu$ is supported in a spatial box of side $O(L^2)$ and in $|t|\leq L^2$, that $\mu(B_r)\leq mr^2$ for every ball of radius $r\geq1$, and that <a href="#eq:src6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src6">[eq:src6]</a> holds for every label, with $1\leq D\leq L$. Then, for every $g>0$, $$\label{eq:src12}
 \mu(A)\leq C mL^4D^{-\sigma}g^{-p},
 \qquad A=\{z:|U(z)|\geq\lambda\},\qquad
 \lambda=gL^{-4/3}.$$ The constant is uniform in $L,D,g,m$ and the size of the array. It may depend on the fixed packet class, including sufficiently many finite profile derivative bounds. For an arbitrary coefficient array, the right side is multiplied by $\|c\|_{\ell^2}^p$.*

</div>

We fix the packet class throughout the proof. In particular, its frequency square, phase-space counting constant, profile support, and required derivative bounds are fixed. Recursive cap scalings will be performed in the original, unrotated coordinates, using squares and retaining only the specified subarray. This preserves the class, including its derivative bounds. Rotations used later are only in nonrecursive estimates. The regularity order can be chosen after all small exponents: the saving $c_s$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:sparse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sparse">25</a>, the geometric powers in Proposition <a href="#prop:rich" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rich">39</a>, and the separation power in Theorem <a href="#thm:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:frame">28</a> do not increase when additional profile regularity is required.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Parameters and recursive estimates

<a id="eq:dyadic-gamma"></a>
Write $c_s$ for the saving in <a href="#eq:src7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src7">[eq:src7]</a>, and $p_0>2$ for the exponent in <a href="#eq:src9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src9">[eq:src9]</a> and <a href="#eq:src10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src10">[eq:src10]</a>. Choose $2<p_{\max}<\min(p_0,3)$ and then $\eta>0$ so small that $$\label{eq:dyadic-gamma}
 \gamma_0=1-p_{\max}/3-p_{\max}\eta>0.$$ We introduce the parameters used in the construction and work conditionally on the inequalities below. Their compatible order of choice will be checked after the estimates, when the losses determining them have been identified. For fixed constants $B,C_a$ and a small exponent $u>0$, set <a id="eq:dyadic-parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:dyadic-parameters}
 X=BD,\qquad D_*=BD^2,\qquad \delta_0\asymp X^{-u},
 \qquad a\asymp X^{C_a},\qquad h=L/a.$$ Round $\delta_0$ and $h$ to convenient dyadic scales, changing only fixed constants. The parameter $D_*$ will be used in the sparse-packet deletion; $\delta_0$ is the upper scale for cap recursion. The shorter packet scale $h$ will be used after the geometric reduction.

<a id="eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions"></a>
We split the proof at $D=L^\kappa$, with $\kappa>0$ sufficiently small. In the range $D<L^\kappa$, we require $h$ to dominate the fixed powers of $X$ occurring below, after enlarging a fixed base scale $L_0$. We also require $2<p\leq p_{\max}$ and $0<\sigma\leq1$ with $$\label{eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions}
 \sigma+\frac{4(p-2)}{3\kappa}<c_s,\qquad
 C_fu+C_g(p-2)+\sigma<c_s,\qquad
 \sigma<u\gamma_0.$$ Here $C_g$ is the fixed exponent separating the large levels $g\geq X^{C_g}$ from the remaining levels, and $C_f$ is a fixed loss exponent, independent of $u$. The frame application below will determine a sufficient value of $C_f$ for <a href="#eq:dyadic-final-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-final-energy">[eq:dyadic-final-energy]</a>. All three inequalities have strict margins. We take $B$ sufficiently large for the recursive terms to contract, and choose the overall induction constant only after the nonrecursive estimates have been obtained.

<a id="eq:dyadic-sup"></a>
Two elementary estimates start the induction. At a point, compact support allows only $O(L^2)$ labels, each atom is $O(L^{-1})$, and hence $$\label{eq:dyadic-sup}
 \|U\|_\infty\lesssim\|c\|_{\ell^2}.$$ Also $\mu(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu)\lesssim mL^4$. Thus all bounded scales are covered by a constant depending on $L_0$. When $D\geq L^\kappa$, unit-cube mass is $O(m)$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:sparse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sparse">25</a> gives $$\int|U|^2\,d\mu
 \leq C m^{1/3}\sum_q\mu(q)^{2/3}\sup_q|U|^2
 \lesssim_\kappa mL^{4/3}D^{-c_s}.$$ Chebyshev therefore gives $CmL^4D^{-c_s}g^{-2}$. A nonempty level set has $g\lesssim L^{4/3}$ by <a href="#eq:dyadic-sup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-sup">[eq:dyadic-sup]</a>; the first inequality in <a href="#eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions">[eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions]</a> implies $D^\sigma g^{p-2}\lesssim D^{c_s}$. This proves <a href="#eq:src12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src12">[eq:src12]</a> in the sparse range independently of induction.

For $D<L^\kappa$, use outer induction on dyadic ranges of $L$ and inner induction on the number of iterations of $d\mapsto Bd^2$ needed to reach $L^\kappa$. Thus the result is available at a fixed smaller packet scale, and at the present scale with parameter $D_*$. For sufficiently large $L$ in this range, $D_*\leq L$; it suffices to have $BL^{2\kappa}\leq L$. Denote the inductive constant by $C_{\rm ind}$. We will bound recursive contributions by a fixed fraction of $C_{\rm ind}mL^4D^{-\sigma}g^{-p}$ and all other contributions by a constant independent of $C_{\rm ind}$.

<a id="stmt-lem-dyadic-cap-gain"></a>
<div id="lem:dyadic-cap-gain" class="lemma">

**Lemma 42** (Cap gain). *For any subarray in a frequency square of side comparable to $r$, where $L^{-1}\lesssim r\leq\delta_0$, outer induction gives <a id="eq:dyadic-cap-gain"></a>$$\label{eq:dyadic-cap-gain}
 \mu\{|U_{\mathcal C}|\geq r^\eta\lambda\}
 \leq C_{\rm cap}C_{\rm ind}mL^4g^{-p}
 r^{1-p/3-p\eta}
 \Big(\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal C}|c_\alpha|^2\Big)^{p/2}.$$ The estimate holds for any submeasure of $\mu$, with the same upper ball parameter. At the smallest scales choose comparable squares so that the child packet size is at least $1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dyadic-cap-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dyadic-cap-gain</summary>

*Proof.* For a square centered at $v$, use $(x',t')=(r(x-2vt),r^2t)$, up to fixed normalization conventions. The child packet size is $L'=rL$, the field factor is $r$, and the pushed measure has ball parameter $m'\leq Cm r^{-3}$. The new threshold parameter is comparable to $g r^{1/3+\eta}$, since $$r^{-1}r^\eta gL^{-4/3}
   =g r^{1/3+\eta}(rL)^{-4/3}.$$ After a fixed inflation of $m'$, covering tubes and their transverse annuli supplies <a href="#eq:src6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src6">[eq:src6]</a> with $D=1$; equivalently $m'(L')^3\asymp mL^3$. The time interval is still $|t'|\leq(L')^2$. Partition the transformed spatial range into boxes of side comparable to $(L')^2$. A compact child packet meets only boundedly many such boxes during this time interval. If their relevant coefficient energies are $e_j$, then $\sum_j e_j\lesssim\sum_{\mathcal C}|c_\alpha|^2$ and $\sum_j e_j^{p/2}\leq(\sum_j e_j)^{p/2}$. Consequently outer induction costs no number of boxes. Its dimensional factor is $$(mr^{-3})(rL)^4(g r^{1/3+\eta})^{-p}
  =mL^4g^{-p}r^{1-p/3-p\eta},$$ as asserted. These parabolic transformations translate the pairs $(b_\alpha/L,L\omega_\alpha)$ and leave the normalized profiles unchanged. Enclosing a specified cap in a comparable square does not add any other labels. ◻

</details>

We need the same estimates when the output point determines a prefix of the array. The next elementary device avoids a loss depending on the number of labels. It adapts the dyadic input-energy decomposition of Christ and Kiselev <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[CK01](#ref-CK01), Theorem 1.1 and its proof in Section 2] to the weak-type block estimates on restricted submeasures used here. We give the proof for these hypotheses, rather than applying their maximal theorem as a black box.

<a id="stmt-lem-dyadic-prefix"></a>
<div id="lem:dyadic-prefix" class="lemma">

**Lemma 43** (Energy truncation). *Let a finite coefficient array be ordered, with total energy $E$, and let each output point select a prefix ending between whole coefficients. Suppose the distribution estimate for every block, on the submeasure where that block is selected, has right side $K t^{-p}\|c_{\rm block}\|_{\ell^2}^p$, where $p>2$. The field formed from the selected prefix then satisfies the same estimate with right side $C_p Kt^{-p}E^{p/2}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dyadic-prefix"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dyadic-prefix</summary>

*Proof.* Zero coefficients may be omitted, and $E=0$ is trivial. Place successive intervals of lengths $|c_\alpha|^2/E$ in $[0,1]$. For a dyadic interval $I\subset[0,1]$, apportion $c_\alpha$ by the fraction of its interval lying in $I$. If that fraction is $f_{\alpha,I}$, its apportioned energy obeys $$\sum_\alpha |f_{\alpha,I}c_\alpha|^2
 \leq\sum_\alpha f_{\alpha,I}|c_\alpha|^2=E|I|.$$ These apportioned blocks satisfy the assumed distribution estimate, up to a constant depending on $p$. Each is either a single apportioned coefficient or the sum of a whole interior block and at most two fractional endpoint coefficients. Homogeneity gives the estimate for the fractional singletons. Split the threshold equally among these at most three pieces and use $\sum_i\|c_i\|_2^p\leq(\sum_i\|c_i\|_2^2)^{p/2}$; the pieces have disjoint coefficient supports. The resulting bound uses exactly the apportioned energy above.

Every prefix interval $[0,s)$ is a disjoint union of at most one dyadic interval at each depth $k\geq0$. Every coefficient contributing to a used interval belongs to the prefix, because $s$ is between whole coefficient intervals. The sum of these block fields equals the prefix field, also when the binary expansion is infinite; the original array is finite and the coefficient fractions sum exactly.

Choose $0<\epsilon<(p/2-1)/p$ and positive thresholds $t_k=c_\epsilon t2^{-\epsilon k}$ with $\sum_k t_k=t$. If a prefix field exceeds $t$, some used block exceeds $t_k$. There are at most $2^k$ blocks at depth $k$, each with energy at most $E2^{-k}$. Restricting the measure separately to points using a block, and summing the distribution bounds, gives $$3^pKt^{-p}E^{p/2}c_\epsilon^{-p}
 \sum_{k\geq0}2^{k(1-p/2+p\epsilon)}
 \leq C_pKt^{-p}E^{p/2}.$$ This argument also applies inside each fixed frequency cap, with its own total energy $E$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Peeling to a common rich measure

<a id="eq:dyadic-angular-law"></a>
Starting with $A$, repeatedly perform the following two deletions. First remove all labels whose smoothed mass against the measure on the current set is less than $mL^3/D_*$. Next remove every point at which the remaining physically neighboring labels do not support a probability law with direction-ball bounds $$\label{eq:dyadic-angular-law}
 \mathbb P\{\omega\in B_r(v)\}
 \leq C_{\rm ang}\delta_0^{-\eta}r^\eta,
 \qquad L^{-1}\leq r\lesssim1.$$ Here a physical neighbor means that the point is within a fixed multiple of $L$ of the packet trajectory; the multiple contains the compact profile support. Directions and frequency centers are interchangeable up to fixed constants on the bounded frequency square.

<a id="eq:dyadic-rich-common"></a>
The angular test can use a finite dyadic label tree, ending at scale comparable to $L^{-1}$, so it depends measurably on one of finitely many incidence patterns. If a round deletes points but no subsequent label, the angular test is unchanged and no further point can be deleted. Since there are finitely many labels, the process terminates. Write $A_\infty$ for its stabilized set and $\mathcal R$ for its surviving labels. At termination all labels in $\mathcal R$ satisfy $$\label{eq:dyadic-rich-common}
 \int_{A_\infty}
 (1+|x-b_\alpha-2\omega_\alpha t|/L)^{-M}\,d\mu
 \geq mL^3/D_*;$$ every point of this same set satisfies <a href="#eq:dyadic-angular-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-angular-law">[eq:dyadic-angular-law]</a> using physically neighboring labels in $\mathcal R$.

<a id="eq:dyadic-prefix-exception"></a>
To estimate what was deleted, order removed labels by their removal round, breaking ties arbitrarily. Each original point selects the prefix removed before it exits, including all labels removed in its exit round; a point of $A_\infty$ selects all removed labels. Let $P(z)$ be this prefix field. For any binary block in Lemma <a href="#lem:dyadic-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dyadic-prefix">43</a>, restrict the measure to points whose prefix uses the block. Such points were still present when each contributing label was removed. By monotonicity of the smoothed integral, every label in that block satisfies <a href="#eq:src6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src6">[eq:src6]</a> with $D_*$. Inner induction and Lemma <a href="#lem:dyadic-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dyadic-prefix">43</a> therefore give, for any fixed small $c>0$, $$\label{eq:dyadic-prefix-exception}
 \mu\{|P|\geq c\lambda\}
 \leq C_p C_{\rm ind}mL^4D_*^{-\sigma}g^{-p}.$$ This restriction of measure is essential: a removed label need not be sparse on all of the original set.

<a id="eq:dyadic-cap-exceptions"></a>
For both the original field and this prefix field, also remove the exceptional sets where a dyadic cap contribution at any scale $L^{-1}\lesssim r\leq\delta_0$ exceeds $r^\eta\lambda$. Lemma <a href="#lem:dyadic-cap-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dyadic-cap-gain">42</a> and, for prefixes, Lemma <a href="#lem:dyadic-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dyadic-prefix">43</a>, imply a total cost $$\label{eq:dyadic-cap-exceptions}
 C' C_{\rm ind}mL^4g^{-p}\delta_0^{\gamma_0}.$$ Indeed cap energies at each scale partition the original energy, so their $p/2$ powers sum to at most $1$, and $1-p/3-p\eta\geq\gamma_0$ makes the dyadic scale sum geometric.

<a id="stmt-lem-dyadic-flow"></a>
<div id="lem:dyadic-flow" class="lemma">

**Lemma 44** (The angular test cannot fail elsewhere). *Outside the exceptions in <a href="#eq:dyadic-prefix-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-prefix-exception">[eq:dyadic-prefix-exception]</a> and <a href="#eq:dyadic-cap-exceptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-cap-exceptions">[eq:dyadic-cap-exceptions]</a>, an original point cannot exit in an angular deletion. On a surviving such point, $|U_{\mathcal R}|\geq c\lambda$ for an absolute $c>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dyadic-flow"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dyadic-flow</summary>

*Proof.* At a proposed exit, subtract the selected prefix from $U$. The remaining field has modulus at least a fixed fraction of $\lambda$, and every fine cap sum has modulus at most $2r^\eta\lambda$. Only physically neighboring remaining labels can contribute at the point.

Mark the terminal cells of the finite direction tree that contain such a neighboring label. Give unmarked terminal cells capacity zero, fine cells of side $r\leq\delta_0$ capacity $r^\eta$, and coarser cells capacity $1$. A cut covering the marked leaves either contains a coarse cell, and hence has capacity at least $1$, or consists of fine cells. In the latter case its cell fields sum to the remaining field, so the triangle inequality gives total capacity at least an absolute positive constant. Nonneighboring labels in these cells contribute zero at the point.

For completeness, the maximal mass that can pass through a node is the minimum of its capacity and the sum of the maximal masses of its children. Induction up the finite tree shows that this is the minimum cut capacity. Thus a mass bounded below can be sent from the root to marked leaves. Choose a neighboring label in each such leaf and normalize the resulting flow to a probability. A fine cell of side $r$ then has probability $O(r^\eta)$. A ball of radius $r\geq L^{-1}$ is covered by boundedly many cells of comparable size. At coarser scales $r\geq\delta_0$, the desired bound follows from $\delta_0^{-\eta}r^\eta\geq1$. Taking $C_{\rm ang}$ large enough gives <a href="#eq:dyadic-angular-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-angular-law">[eq:dyadic-angular-law]</a>, a contradiction to the exit test. The assertion about the final field follows by subtracting its prefix. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:dyadic-contraction"></a>
Relative to the target with constant $C_{\rm ind}$, the recursive costs so far are bounded by fixed multiples of $$\label{eq:dyadic-contraction}
 (D_*/D)^{-\sigma}=X^{-\sigma},\qquad
 D^\sigma\delta_0^{\gamma_0}
 \lesssim D^\sigma X^{-u\gamma_0}.$$ The later single-cap exceptions have the second form as well. The last inequality in <a href="#eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions">[eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions]</a> and a sufficiently large $B$ make the sum of all these costs less than, say, half the target. Their constants include the fixed prefix constant $C_p$, but do not include any of the final nonrecursive constants.

We apply geometry to $\mu|_{A_\infty}$ itself, before subtracting any of the proof-only exceptional sets. Equations <a href="#eq:dyadic-angular-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-angular-law">[eq:dyadic-angular-law]</a> and <a href="#eq:dyadic-rich-common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-rich-common">[eq:dyadic-rich-common]</a> therefore hold for the same measure. Subsequent upper bounds may use the original $\mu$: its ball and sparsity upper bounds still hold for every surviving label.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### Plates and coarse frequency intervals

Divide physical coordinates by $L$, and divide $\mu|_{A_\infty}$ by $mL^2$. In the notation of Proposition <a href="#prop:rich" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rich">39</a>, $H=L$, the richness is at least $L/D_*\geq L/X^2$, and the angular constant in <a href="#eq:dyadic-angular-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-angular-law">[eq:dyadic-angular-law]</a> is at most a fixed power of $X$. The required comparisons $X^C\ll H$ hold by the choice of $\kappa$. Proposition <a href="#prop:rich" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rich">39</a> assigns every surviving tube to one of at most $X^{C_1}$ plates of width $X^{C_1}$ in these coordinates, with $$|n\cdot(2\omega,1)|\leq X^{C_1}/L.$$ Enlarge the plates by a fixed polynomial factor to contain the physical packet supports in the domain. All assignments are disjoint at the level of coefficient labels.

<a id="eq:dyadic-large-level"></a>
<a id="eq:dyadic-middle-levels"></a>
The spatial component $n_x$ of such a unit normal is bounded below. Indeed $|\omega|\leq C$, so if $|n_x|$ were sufficiently small, then $|n_t+2n_x\cdot\omega|$ would be bounded below by an absolute constant, contrary to the displayed estimate for large $L$. Thus the assigned frequencies lie in a strip of width $X^{O(1)}/L$, which can be partitioned into polynomially many strips of width $O(L^{-1})$. If the total number of resulting arrays is $N\leq X^{C_2}$, the triangle inequality and Proposition <a href="#prop:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:strip">37</a> give $$\label{eq:dyadic-large-level}
 \mu\{|U_{\mathcal R}|\geq c\lambda\}
 \lesssim mL^4N^{p_0}g^{-p_0}
       \sum_{j=1}^N\|c_j\|_{\ell^2}^{p_0}
 \leq X^{C_3}mL^4g^{-p_0}.$$ Choosing $C_g(p_0-p_{\max})>C_3+1$ makes this at most a fixed multiple of the target whenever $g\geq X^{C_g}$. For $g<D^{-1}$, total mass suffices, since $D^{-\sigma}g^{-p}\geq D^{p-\sigma}\geq1$ after decreasing $\sigma$ if necessary. We henceforth work in $$\label{eq:dyadic-middle-levels}
 D^{-1}\leq g\leq X^{C_g}.$$

<a id="eq:dyadic-plate-discard"></a>
Discard intersections of enlarged plates whose projective normal directions differ by at least a constant multiple of $a/L$. For two plates of normalized width $w\leq X^{C_4}$ and normal separation $\beta$, their intersection is in a box of length $O(L)$ and transverse sides $O(w)$ and $O(w/\beta)$. Its normalized mass is at most $X^{O(1)}L/\beta$ by unit-ball covering. Summing over the polynomial plate list, the discarded mass in the original coordinates is at most $$\label{eq:dyadic-plate-discard}
 mL^4X^{C_5}/a.$$ Choose $C_a>C_5+1+C_gp_{\max}$, with room for fixed constants. Then <a href="#eq:dyadic-plate-discard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-plate-discard">[eq:dyadic-plate-discard]</a> is bounded by the target throughout <a href="#eq:dyadic-middle-levels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-middle-levels">[eq:dyadic-middle-levels]</a>.

Partition projective normal directions into cells of diameter comparable to $a/L$, with bounded overlap on balls of that radius. At a retained point all active plate normals have pairwise distance $O(a/L)$. Consequently only boundedly many normal cells contribute, and at least one cluster field has modulus at least $c\lambda$. Relative to a representative normal in each nonempty cell, its label frequencies lie in a strip of width $O(a/L)=O(h^{-1})$. The representative still has spatial component bounded below, and this follows by perturbing the previous direction inequality by $O(a/L)$. The clusters partition the surviving coefficient energy.

For each cluster separately rotate the spatial coordinates and remove its central normal frequency by a bounded shear and modulation. Write the resulting coordinates as $(x,y,t)$, with $x$ tangential and $y$ normal. Partition tangential label frequencies into coarse intervals $\tau$ of length comparable to $\delta_0$, then into fine intervals $\theta$ of length comparable to $h^{-1}$, using nested grids and consistent endpoint assignments. Each coarse array fits in a comparable two-dimensional cap, because $h^{-1}\ll\delta_0$. In applying Lemma <a href="#lem:dyadic-cap-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dyadic-cap-gain">42</a>, enclose that array in an unrotated square in the original coordinates, keeping only its labels. At each scale bounded overlap of the enclosing squares, together with the partitioned coefficient energy across clusters, gives a total exceptional cost $$C''C_{\rm ind}mL^4g^{-p}\delta_0^{\gamma_0}$$ for the sets on which a coarse cluster field exceeds $\delta_0^\eta\lambda$. This is the further recursive cost already allowed in <a href="#eq:dyadic-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-contraction">[eq:dyadic-contraction]</a>.

<a id="eq:dyadic-significant-intervals"></a>
At any remaining point where a cluster is high, at least $c\delta_0^{-\eta}$ distinct coarse intervals satisfy $$\label{eq:dyadic-significant-intervals}
 |U_\tau|\geq c'\delta_0\lambda.$$ To see this, there are $O(\delta_0^{-1})$ coarse intervals, so the sum of those below the displayed small threshold has modulus less than half the cluster threshold if $c'$ is sufficiently small. Each of the others has modulus at most $\delta_0^\eta\lambda$, forcing the stated number. Full partition intervals are used throughout; the complement estimate never discards only part of an interval field.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Flattening all intermediate scales

The cluster frequencies now lie in a strip of width $O(h^{-1})$, whereas Proposition <a href="#prop:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:strip">37</a> at packet size $L$ requires width $O(L^{-1})$. Splitting into thinner strips produced the large-level estimate, but paid a fixed power of $X$. To handle the remaining levels, we instead make one smooth packet out of each entire fine frequency array on each short physical cell. We will apply Theorem <a href="#thm:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:frame">28</a> directly to these packets and sum their energies. The construction uses only the general compact profiles in <a href="#eq:src5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src5">[eq:src5]</a>; no equation or exact Fourier support is imposed on those profiles.

<a id="eq:dyadic-child-coordinates"></a>
For a fine interval $\theta$ centered at $v_\theta$, put $$\label{eq:dyadic-child-coordinates}
 z'_\theta=F_\theta(x,y,t)
   =\big((x-2v_\theta t)/h,\ y/h,\ t/h^2\big).$$ The central normal frequency has already been removed. The array is contained in a cap of size $O(h^{-1})$ and therefore has the form <a id="eq:dyadic-child-field"></a>$$\label{eq:dyadic-child-field}
 U_\theta(x,y,t)
 =h^{-1}e^{i(xv_\theta-tv_\theta^2)}U'_\theta(z'_\theta),$$ up to a common harmless modulation. Here $U'_\theta$ is an array of packet size $a=L/h$, with bounded frequencies and the original coefficient norm.

<a id="eq:dyadic-density"></a>
Choose a fixed smooth partition of unity $\{\psi_T\}$ on the unit lattice in $\mathbb R^3$, with bounded overlap and fixed enlarged supports. Take a finite derivative order $N$ exceeding all later Sobolev and frame requirements, and let $$s_{\theta,T}
   =h^{-1}\max_{|\beta|\leq N}
       \sup_{z'\in T^*}|\partial^\beta U'_\theta(z')|,$$ where $T^*$ is a fixed sufficiently large enlargement of $T$. Enlarging the fixed constant in this definition would give the same estimates. Fix also a large polynomial decay power $P$, larger than $M+3$ and all tail moments that will occur, and define $$\label{eq:dyadic-density}
 d_\theta(T)=\frac{1}{mh^3}
   \int(1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(F_\theta(z),T))^{-P}\,d\mu(z).$$ We abbreviate this to $d(T)$ when the cluster and $\theta$ are specified. All coordinate changes push forward the measure without changing its mass. Their ball constants are uniformly bounded after the displayed factor $h^3$. In particular $d(T)\lesssim1$ by annular covering.

<a id="eq:dyadic-composite-packet"></a>
Only cells whose partition supports meet the testing domain need be retained. The associated summand $$\label{eq:dyadic-composite-packet}
 e^{i(xv_\theta-tv_\theta^2)}
 h^{-1}\psi_T(z'_\theta)U'_\theta(z'_\theta)$$ is a single composite packet: it has tangential width $O(h)$, time length $O(h^2)$, localized dependence on $y/h$, and all required normalized derivatives bounded by $Cs_{\theta,T}$.

There are two energy bounds to retain. On a time interval of length $h^2$, spatial almost orthogonality controls the total squared amplitudes of the composite packets. Over the full time range, the sparse estimate controls the same amplitudes weighted by $d(T)^{2/3}$. The first bound will supply the large threshold-to-envelope ratios required by the frame theorem; the second will retain the factor $D^{-c_s}$ when the frame estimates are summed.

<a id="stmt-lem-dyadic-flattening"></a>
<div id="lem:dyadic-flattening" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:src13"></a>
**Lemma 45** (Flattened energy estimates). *For a time interval $J$ of length $h^2$, let $\mathcal T(J)$ denote the boundedly many native time-cell layers that can contribute on $J$. Summing over the disjoint fine arrays, and also over clusters when needed, one has $$\label{eq:src13}
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\theta,\,T\in\mathcal T(J)}s_{\theta,T}^2
     &\lesssim h^{-2},\\
 \sum_{\theta,T}d(T)^{2/3}s_{\theta,T}^2
     &\lesssim_B h^{-2}a^{4/3}D^{-c_s}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The right sides are multiplied by the total original coefficient energy if it is not normalized to be at most $1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dyadic-flattening"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dyadic-flattening</summary>

<a id="eq:dyadic-gram"></a>
*Proof.* We first justify spatial almost orthogonality for arbitrary smooth packet profiles. At native time $t'=O(a^2)$, a child atom and all its fixed derivatives have spatial support within $O(a)$ of its transported intercept $b'_\alpha+2\omega'_\alpha t'$. Spatial integration by parts in the difference of the carrier frequencies gives Gram entries bounded by $$\label{eq:dyadic-gram}
 C_N(1+a|\omega'_\alpha-\omega'_\beta|)^{-N},$$ and the entries vanish unless the transported intercepts differ by $O(a)$. The joint centers $$(b'_\alpha/a,a\omega'_\alpha)
 \longmapsto
 \big(b'_\alpha/a+2(t'/a^2)a\omega'_\alpha,
      a\omega'_\alpha\big)$$ undergo a uniformly bounded shear. Thus their phase-space counts remain bounded. Summing <a href="#eq:dyadic-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-gram">[eq:dyadic-gram]</a> over these centers gives uniformly summable Gram rows when $N$ is sufficiently large. It follows that, for every required derivative, $$\|\partial^\beta U'_\theta(\cdot,t')\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}^2
 \lesssim \sum_{\alpha\in\theta}|c_\alpha|^2.$$ Time derivatives are included: bounded child frequencies and factors $a^{-1}$ or $a^{-2}$ from differentiating profiles preserve the same packet bounds. Unit-cell Sobolev estimates, bounded overlap of the enlarged cells, and integration over the $O(1)$ native time layers in $J$ now give the first assertion of <a href="#eq:src13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src13">[eq:src13]</a>. Summing the coefficient energies across fine arrays and clusters costs at most $1$.

<a id="eq:dyadic-child-moment"></a>
For the second assertion, let $\mu_\theta=F_{\theta\#}\mu$ in the coordinates of the relevant cluster. Covering the inverse image of a ball shows that its ball parameter is at most $Cmh^3$ for radii at least $1$. For every child label, the transverse ratio in <a href="#eq:src6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src6">[eq:src6]</a> is unchanged by <a href="#eq:dyadic-child-coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-child-coordinates">[eq:dyadic-child-coordinates]</a>, and $$\label{eq:dyadic-child-moment}
 (mh^3)a^3/D=mL^3/D.$$ Thus $\mu_\theta$ satisfies the same sparsity condition with $D$ after a fixed inflation of its ball parameter.

<a id="eq:dyadic-convolved-cell"></a>
Convolve $\mu_\theta$ with $K_P(z')=(1+|z'|)^{-P}$ and restrict the resulting measure to $|t'|\leq C'a^2$, with a fixed enlargement sufficient for the cells and derivatives in use. Call the resulting measure $\widetilde\mu_\theta$. Its ball parameter remains $Cmh^3$. Its smoothed tube moments are still bounded as in <a href="#eq:dyadic-child-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-child-moment">[eq:dyadic-child-moment]</a>: translating a child tube kernel by $w$ changes it by at most a factor $C(1+|w|)^M$, and $$\int_{\mathbb R^3}(1+|w|)^{M-P}\,dw<\infty.$$ Every relevant unit cell $q$ in a fixed enlargement of $T$ has $$\label{eq:dyadic-convolved-cell}
 \widetilde\mu_\theta(q)\gtrsim mh^3d(T),$$ after using the time enlargement; this follows by comparing the kernel at all points of $q$ with its distance to $T$.

Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:sparse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sparse">25</a>, including its derivative version, at packet size $a$ to $U'_\theta$ and $\widetilde\mu_\theta$. Unit cells in $T^*$, of which there are boundedly many, control the suprema defining $s_{\theta,T}$. Using <a href="#eq:dyadic-convolved-cell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-convolved-cell">[eq:dyadic-convolved-cell]</a> and dividing out $(mh^3)^{2/3}$ yields $$\sum_T d(T)^{2/3}s_{\theta,T}^2
 \lesssim h^{-2}a^{4/3}D^{-c_s}
       \sum_{\alpha\in\theta}|c_\alpha|^2.$$ The transformed spatial domain may exceed size $a^2$. To use the local form of the sparse estimate, divide it into boxes of side comparable to $a^2$ and retain only intersecting compact child packets. Each such packet meets boundedly many boxes on $|t'|\leq C'a^2$; summing the squared estimates therefore uses only bounded total coefficient energy.

This application requires a fixed positive-power lower bound on $D$ in terms of $a$. By $a\asymp(BD)^{C_a}$ and $a\geq D$, we have $a^{1/(2C_a+2)}\leq D\leq a$ once $D$ exceeds a constant depending on $B$. Use this fixed exponent in Proposition <a href="#prop:sparse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sparse">25</a>. For the remaining bounded values of $D$, also $a$ is bounded in terms of $B$. The first assertion summed over $O(a^2)$ time layers, and $d(T)\lesssim1$, gives the second assertion with a different allowed constant. Finally sum over the partitioned coefficient arrays. ◻

</details>

We sort the cells into dyadic density bands $d(T)\asymp d_\ell$, where $d_\ell=d_{\max}2^{-\ell}$, $\ell\geq0$, and $d_{\max}$ is a fixed upper bound. Shifting this index by a fixed amount has no effect. Only $O(\log h)$ bands need occur. Indeed if the total measure of the domain is already less than $mL^4D^{-\sigma}g^{-p}$ there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, for a relevant cell the transformed domain diameter is $O(L^2/h)$, and the strictly positive weight in <a href="#eq:dyadic-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-density">[eq:dyadic-density]</a> gives <a id="eq:dyadic-density-lower"></a>$$\label{eq:dyadic-density-lower}
 d(T)\gtrsim (mh^3)^{-1}\mu(\text{domain})
                   (1+CL^2/h)^{-P}
 \gtrsim a^{4-2P}h^{1-P}D^{-\sigma}g^{-p}.$$ Here $L=ah$, and fixed powers of $a,D,g$ are bounded by fixed powers of $X$, which in turn are bounded by fixed powers of $h$. Thus $d(T)\geq c_B h^{-C}$ with a fixed $C$. This band count will only be used with a power gain in $h$; it will not remain in the final bound.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Two bands and the bilinear frame estimate

<a id="eq:dyadic-band-level"></a>
For every coarse interval satisfying <a href="#eq:dyadic-significant-intervals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-significant-intervals">[eq:dyadic-significant-intervals]</a>, summably split its threshold over the density bands. At least one band field then has modulus at least $$\label{eq:dyadic-band-level}
 A_\ell=c\delta_0(1+\ell)^{-2}\lambda.$$ The constant $c>0$ is fixed sufficiently small that the sum of these thresholds is less than the significant-interval threshold. Choose one such band for each significant interval. Fix a sufficiently large exponent $C_d$, to be chosen in the tiny-band estimate below. If one selected band has $d<X^{-C_d}$, pair its interval with a separated significant interval. Otherwise there are only $O(C_d\log X)$ selected bands. By increasing $B$, the number $c\delta_0^{-\eta}$ in <a href="#eq:dyadic-significant-intervals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-significant-intervals">[eq:dyadic-significant-intervals]</a> exceeds a sufficiently large multiple of this band count. Pigeonholing gives two separated coarse intervals with the same band. Here “separated” means distance at least a fixed multiple of $\delta_0$; excluding a bounded number of neighboring intervals suffices. The list of significant intervals is also large enough to supply the partner in the tiny-band case.

<a id="eq:dyadic-class-energy"></a>
Partition time into intervals $J$ of length $h^2$. For each cluster, $J$, separated pair $(\tau_1,\tau_2)$, and selected bands $(\ell_1,\ell_2)$, form the measurable subset where both band fields exceed the corresponding levels in <a href="#eq:dyadic-band-level" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-band-level">[eq:dyadic-band-level]</a>. Restrict to the high points under consideration if desired. Denote its mass by $M_0$, and put $$\label{eq:dyadic-class-energy}
 E_i=\sum_{\substack{\theta\subset\tau_i,\ T\in\mathcal T(J)\\
                         d(T)\asymp d_i}}
                 s_{\theta,T}^2,
 \qquad d_i=d_{\ell_i},\qquad i=1,2.$$ The witness subsets need not be disjoint; we use an upper bound on their sum of masses. There are at most $X^{C_6}(\log h)^2$ relevant tuples: the cluster list and interval pairs have polynomial size in $X$, and the time partition contributes $O(a^2)$ intervals.

<a id="eq:dyadic-small-mass"></a>
Tuples with $$\label{eq:dyadic-small-mass}
 M_0\leq mh^{4-1/12}$$ have total mass at most $X^{C_6}(\log h)^2mh^{4-1/12}$. Relative to the target this is at most $$C X^{C_6+\sigma+C_gp_{\max}}
          a^{-4}h^{-1/12}(\log h)^2,$$ which is bounded, and can be made small at large scales, by the choice of $\kappa$. This discard counts tuples before normal spatial slabs are introduced, so it incurs no number of slabs.

<a id="eq:dyadic-markov"></a>
<a id="eq:dyadic-ratios"></a>
Fix a remaining tuple. Define its two full square envelopes by $$S_i(z)^2=
 \sum_{\substack{\theta\subset\tau_i,\ T\in\mathcal T(J)\\
                         d(T)\asymp d_i}}
 s_{\theta,T}^2(1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(F_\theta(z),T))^{-P}.$$ By <a href="#eq:dyadic-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-density">[eq:dyadic-density]</a>, $\int S_i^2\,d\mu\lesssim mh^3d_iE_i$. Markov’s inequality retains at least half the witness mass with both bounds $$\label{eq:dyadic-markov}
 S_i\leq B_i,
 \qquad B_i=C\left(\frac{mh^3d_iE_i}{M_0}\right)^{1/2}.$$ The constant $C$ here is large and absolute. The first estimate in <a href="#eq:src13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src13">[eq:src13]</a>, $d_i\lesssim1$, and the reverse of <a href="#eq:dyadic-small-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-small-mass">[eq:dyadic-small-mass]</a> give $$\label{eq:dyadic-ratios}
 B_i\lesssim h^{-3/2+1/24},\qquad
 \frac{A_{\ell_i}}{B_i}
 \gtrsim h^{1/8}\delta_0g a^{-4/3}(1+\ell_i)^{-2}.$$ Since $g\geq D^{-1}$ and $\ell_i=O(\log h)$, the right side of the ratio estimate is bounded below by $h^{1/8}$ divided by a fixed power of $X$ and by $C_B(\log h)^2$. Our scale hierarchy makes it larger than the fixed threshold-ratio constant in Theorem <a href="#thm:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:frame">28</a>. The same hierarchy ensures $\delta_0\geq h^{-1/2}$. Zero class energy would give an identically zero class field, so it cannot occur in a tuple with positive witness mass.

<a id="stmt-lem-dyadic-tuple"></a>
<div id="lem:dyadic-tuple" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:dyadic-frame-substitution"></a>
<a id="eq:dyadic-cubic-mass"></a>
**Lemma 46** (Frame estimate for one tuple). *For every tuple remaining after <a href="#eq:dyadic-small-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-small-mass">[eq:dyadic-small-mass]</a>, $$\label{eq:dyadic-frame-substitution}
 M_0\lesssim \delta_0^{-C}mh
 \frac{B_1^2B_2^2}{A_{\ell_1}^2A_{\ell_2}^2}
 \left(\frac{h^3E_1}{A_{\ell_1}^2}
            +\frac{h^3E_2}{A_{\ell_2}^2}\right).$$ In particular, $$\label{eq:dyadic-cubic-mass}
 \lambda^6M_0^3\lesssim
 \delta_0^{-C}(1+\ell_1+\ell_2)^C
 m^3h^{10}d_1d_2E_1E_2(E_1+E_2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dyadic-tuple"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dyadic-tuple</summary>

<a id="eq:dyadic-recentered-intercept"></a>
*Proof.* Partition the normal coordinate into slabs of width $h$. In a slab centered at $y_0$, only boundedly many normal cell indices $T_y$ can contribute, and on $J$ only boundedly many time indices $T_t$ can contribute. Write $t=t_0+s$ with $t_0$ an endpoint of $J$. For a composite cell in <a href="#eq:dyadic-composite-packet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-composite-packet">[eq:dyadic-composite-packet]</a>, the tangential carrier has frequency $v_\theta$, and its intercept in the time coordinate $s$ is $$\label{eq:dyadic-recentered-intercept}
 b_{\theta,T}=hT_x+2v_\theta t_0.$$ The fine centers are spaced at scale $h^{-1}$. At each such center, $T_x$ runs over an integer lattice translated by $2v_\theta t_0/h$; the bounded number of normal and time indices thus gives the required one-dimensional phase-space counts uniformly in $t_0$. The packet profiles are compact and smooth on the $h,h^2$ scales, by the definition of $s_{\theta,T}$.

Expand these profiles, with a fixed smooth cutoff on an enlargement of the slab, in the variable $(y-y_0)/h$. The mode packets can be written with scalar amplitudes at most $$C s_{\theta,T}\langle n\rangle^{-N_1},
 \qquad \langle n\rangle=(1+n^2)^{1/2},$$ and uniformly bounded required tangential profile derivatives, for a fixed large $N_1$. This follows by integrating the Fourier coefficients by parts, using enough derivatives in the definition of $s$. The external Fourier phases have modulus one at every witness.

For the cells belonging to this slab and time bin, the omitted normal and native time displacements in $F_\theta(z)-T$ are bounded at a witness. Hence the one-dimensional envelope weight in Theorem <a href="#thm:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:frame">28</a>, after <a href="#eq:dyadic-recentered-intercept" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-recentered-intercept">[eq:dyadic-recentered-intercept]</a>, is at most a fixed multiple of the full weight defining $S_i$ there. On the set retained in <a href="#eq:dyadic-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-markov">[eq:dyadic-markov]</a>, the $n$th mode envelope is consequently bounded by $CB_i\langle n\rangle^{-N_1}$. Its energy parameter $H_i^*$ in <a href="#eq:src8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src8">[eq:src8]</a> is at most $Ch^3E_{i,\mathrm{slab}}\langle n\rangle^{-2N_1}$, where $E_{i,\mathrm{slab}}$ is the part of <a href="#eq:dyadic-class-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-class-energy">[eq:dyadic-class-energy]</a> near this slab.

Whenever a band field has modulus at least $A_{\ell_i}$, some Fourier mode has modulus at least $cA_{\ell_i}\langle n\rangle^{-2}$. For each pair of selected modes apply Theorem <a href="#thm:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:frame">28</a> at separation $\delta_0$. The threshold-to-envelope ratios only improve with $|n|$ when $N_1>2$, so <a href="#eq:dyadic-ratios" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-ratios">[eq:dyadic-ratios]</a> verifies its ratio hypothesis uniformly in the modes. The factors in the frame estimate are summable over the two mode indices: for example its first energy term has powers $\langle n_1\rangle^{-4N_1+8}
 \langle n_2\rangle^{-2N_1+4}$. Take $N_1>3$, with sufficient additional regularity for all derivatives.

The projection to $(x,t)$ of the measure restricted to a slab has mass at most $Cmh$ on any unit square: its inverse image has dimensions $O(1),O(h),O(1)$ and is covered by $O(h)$ unit balls. This is an upper bound on the full projected measure, regardless of which normal coordinate supplied a witness. Multiplying the unit-square count from <a href="#eq:src8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src8">[eq:src8]</a> by $Cmh$, and summing the modes, bounds the retained witness mass in this slab by $$C\delta_0^{-C}mh
 \frac{B_1^2B_2^2}{A_{\ell_1}^2A_{\ell_2}^2}
 \left(\frac{h^3E_{1,\mathrm{slab}}}{A_{\ell_1}^2}
       +\frac{h^3E_{2,\mathrm{slab}}}{A_{\ell_2}^2}\right).$$ Each composite cell belongs to boundedly many enlarged slabs, and therefore $\sum_{\mathrm{slab}}E_{i,\mathrm{slab}}\lesssim E_i$. Summing costs no number of slabs and proves <a href="#eq:dyadic-frame-substitution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-frame-substitution">[eq:dyadic-frame-substitution]</a>, since at least half of $M_0$ was retained by Markov.

Finally substitute <a href="#eq:dyadic-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-markov">[eq:dyadic-markov]</a> and <a href="#eq:dyadic-band-level" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-band-level">[eq:dyadic-band-level]</a>. The product of the two squared envelope bounds supplies $m^2h^6d_1d_2E_1E_2/M_0^2$; the projected square mass and frame energy supply $mh\cdot h^3$. Thus the total factor is $m^3h^{10}/M_0^2$. The six threshold powers contribute $\lambda^{-6}$ times a fixed power of $\delta_0^{-1}$ and of $1+\ell_1+\ell_2$. Rearranging proves <a href="#eq:dyadic-cubic-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-cubic-mass">[eq:dyadic-cubic-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Summing the bands and closing the induction

<a id="eq:dyadic-tiny-band"></a>
First suppose at least one selected band is tiny. The unweighted energy bound $E_i\lesssim h^{-2}$ in <a href="#eq:src13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src13">[eq:src13]</a> and the cube root of <a href="#eq:dyadic-cubic-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-cubic-mass">[eq:dyadic-cubic-mass]</a> give $$\label{eq:dyadic-tiny-band}
 \lambda^2M_0\lesssim
 \delta_0^{-C}(1+\ell_1+\ell_2)^C
 mh^{4/3}(d_1d_2)^{1/3}.$$ For dyadic bands, geometric decay absorbs the polynomial index factors: $$\sum_{\min(d_1,d_2)<X^{-C_d}}
 (1+\ell_1+\ell_2)^C(d_1d_2)^{1/3}
 \lesssim X^{-C_d/6}.$$ The constants here depend on the fixed exponent $C$. After summing over clusters, time bins and coarse interval pairs, all other costs in <a href="#eq:dyadic-tiny-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-tiny-band">[eq:dyadic-tiny-band]</a> are at most a fixed power of $X$, uniformly for $u\leq1$. Choose $C_d$ large enough to absorb this power, together with $D^\sigma g^{p-2}$ for <a href="#eq:dyadic-middle-levels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-middle-levels">[eq:dyadic-middle-levels]</a>; using $h^{4/3}\leq L^{4/3}$, this gives $$\lambda^2\sum_{\rm tiny}M_0
 \lesssim mL^{4/3}D^{-\sigma}g^{-(p-2)}.$$ This is precisely the target multiplied by $\lambda^2$, with a constant independent of induction.

<a id="eq:dyadic-final-energy"></a>
For the other tuples the two density bands coincide, say $d_1\asymp
d_2\asymp d\geq X^{-C_d}$. Then $\ell_i=O(C_d\log X)$, and $$\big(E_1E_2(E_1+E_2)\big)^{1/3}\leq C(E_1+E_2).$$ The cube root of <a href="#eq:dyadic-cubic-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-cubic-mass">[eq:dyadic-cubic-mass]</a> gives <a id="eq:dyadic-common-band"></a>$$\label{eq:dyadic-common-band}
 \lambda^2M_0\lesssim
 \delta_0^{-C}(\log X)^C
 mh^{10/3}d^{2/3}(E_1+E_2).$$ We now sum energies rather than the number of tuples. A composite cell appears only in its own cluster, in boundedly many $J$, in its specified density band and coarse interval, and for at most $O(\delta_0^{-1})$ choices of the other coarse interval at that band. There is consequently only an additional fixed power of $\delta_0^{-1}$ in the multiplicity. In particular, no factor $a^2$ from time bins, no number of clusters, and no number of density bands is paid in this sum. The weighted estimate in <a href="#eq:src13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src13">[eq:src13]</a> therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \lambda^2\sum_{\rm common}M_0
 &\lesssim_B
 X^{C_fu}(\log X)^{C_f}mh^{10/3}
       \big(h^{-2}a^{4/3}D^{-c_s}\big)\notag\\
 &=C_BX^{C_fu}(\log X)^{C_f}
       mL^{4/3}D^{-c_s}.
 \label{eq:dyadic-final-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ The fixed exponent $C_f$ includes the just described partner multiplicity; it is the exponent used in choosing $u$. The transforms vary with $\theta$ and with the cluster, but their coefficient arrays are disjoint, exactly as required in the proof of <a href="#eq:src13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src13">[eq:src13]</a>.

The target after multiplication by $\lambda^2$ is $mL^{4/3}D^{-\sigma}g^{-(p-2)}$.
