Since $g\leq X^{C_g}$, the ratio of <a href="#eq:dyadic-final-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-final-energy">[eq:dyadic-final-energy]</a> to this quantity is at most $$C_BX^{C_fu+C_g(p-2)}(\log X)^{C_f}D^{\sigma-c_s}.$$ It is bounded by a fixed constant depending on $B$ by the strict second inequality in <a href="#eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions">[eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions]</a>. This proves the required estimate for the common-band tuples with no growing loss in $L$ when $D$ and $g$ are fixed.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
###### Compatible order of parameter choices.

We now verify that the conditions used in the proof can be imposed together. The values of $p_{\max}$ and $\eta$ were fixed in <a href="#eq:dyadic-gamma" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-gamma">[eq:dyadic-gamma]</a>. Temporarily restricting $0<u\leq1$ and $0<\sigma\leq1$ bounds all geometric losses by fixed powers of $X$. Choose $C_g$ large enough for <a href="#eq:dyadic-large-level" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-large-level">[eq:dyadic-large-level]</a>, using only these powers and the positive gap $p_0-p_{\max}$. Choose $C_a$ larger still for <a href="#eq:dyadic-plate-discard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-plate-discard">[eq:dyadic-plate-discard]</a>, and then choose $C_d$ for the tiny-band sum. These exponents are independent of how small $u$ will be.

The equal-band estimate <a href="#eq:dyadic-final-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-final-energy">[eq:dyadic-final-energy]</a> has loss $X^{C_fu}(\log X)^{C_f}$, where the fixed $C_f$ accounts for the frame separation loss and the coarse-interval multiplicity. Choose $u>0$ so small that $C_fu<c_s/4$. Next choose $\kappa>0$ small in terms of all the fixed powers just chosen. For any fixed required $K$, the relations $X\leq BL^\kappa$ and $h\asymp L/X^{C_a}$ give $h\geq X^K$ at sufficiently large $L$ if $\kappa(C_a+K)<1$. This supplies the geometric scale conditions, $\delta_0\geq h^{-1/2}$, the frame threshold ratios, and the small-mass discard.

Choose $p>2$ sufficiently close to $2$, with $p\leq p_{\max}$, and then choose $\sigma>0$ sufficiently small to satisfy all three strict inequalities in <a href="#eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions">[eq:dyadic-exponent-conditions]</a>. Finally choose $B$ large enough to contract the recursive terms and to supply the angular pigeonhole argument. Constants depending on $B$ are allowed in the nonrecursive estimates. Enlarge the fixed base scale $L_0$ to cover the remaining comparisons involving $B$. Every fixed constant and exponent is chosen before the induction constant and the variable scales $L,D$.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dyadic-tuple-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dyadic-tuple</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distribution">41</a>.* The sparse range and bounded scales were handled directly. In the remaining range, the prefix exceptions, all dyadic-cap exceptions, and the coarse cluster exceptions cost less than half the desired bound with constant $C_{\rm ind}$, by <a href="#eq:dyadic-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-contraction">[eq:dyadic-contraction]</a>. Every other original high point either is handled by the large-level strip estimate or total mass, lies in a discarded plate intersection, belongs to a small-mass tuple, or is covered by the tiny-band or common-band bounds. All these latter costs are bounded by a fixed multiple of $mL^4D^{-\sigma}g^{-p}$ independently of $C_{\rm ind}$. Choose $C_{\rm ind}$ once to be twice their total constant and large enough for the initial cases. The two inductions close. Homogeneity supplies the factor $\|c\|_{\ell^2}^p$ for arbitrary subarrays and completes the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:endpoint"></a>
### Endpoint square summation and the order of limits

The exponent $p>2$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distribution">41</a> has a final, essential use. It supplies a positive convexity deficit on a common dyadic time tree. Combined with spatial localization, this gives a square sum over frequency scales and hence the Sobolev norm at the endpoint. We then construct a representative continuous in time and verify that the prescribed Gaussian limits recover that representative at every real time on one full-measure set.

Throughout this section, $S(t)f=e^{it\Delta}f$ denotes the $L^2$ evolution, whose Fourier multiplier is $e^{-it|\xi|^2}$. When $\widehat f$ has compact support, we also use $S(t)f(x)$ for its absolutely convergent Fourier integral. This is continuous in $(x,t)$, since then $\widehat f\in L^1$. Constants may depend on fixed annular supports and smooth cutoff functions, but are independent of frequency, the center of a spatial ball, and every measurable choice of time made below.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### From packet distributions to a short-time maximal estimate

For $p>0$ and measurable $h$ on $A\subset\mathbb R^2$, we use the weak $L^p$ quasinorm $$\|h\|_{L^{p,\infty}(A)}
   =\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda
      |\{x\in A:|h(x)|>\lambda\}|^{1/p}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-shortmax"></a>
<div id="prop:shortmax" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:src24"></a>
**Proposition 47** (Short-time maximal estimate). *Let $p>2$ be the exponent in Theorem <a href="#thm:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distribution">41</a>. Fix $0<c_0<C_0<\infty$. If $N\ge1$ is dyadic and $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\widehat f_N\subset
 \{\xi\in\mathbb R^2:c_0N\le |\xi|\le C_0N\},$$ then, on every unit ball $B\subset\mathbb R^2$, $$\left\|\sup_{0<t<1/N}|S(t)f_N|\right\|_{L^{p,\infty}(B)}
 \le C N^{1/3}\|f_N\|_2.
 \label{eq:src24}$$ The same estimate holds on every translated time interval of length at most $1/N$, with either endpoint included.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-shortmax"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-shortmax</summary>

*Proof.* We first apply Theorem <a href="#thm:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distribution">41</a> at $D=1$ to an arbitrary measurable spatial graph in unit-frequency variables. Let $\mathcal B$ be a spatial ball of radius $O(L^2)$, let $T:\mathcal B\to[0,L^2]$ be measurable, and define <a id="eq:endpoint-graph-measure"></a>$$\mu(F)=\int_{\mathcal B}{\bf1}_F(X,T(X))\,dX.
 \label{eq:endpoint-graph-measure}$$ This is the pushforward of spatial area, regardless of the regularity of $T$. A space-time ball of radius $r$ projects into a spatial disk of the same radius, so $\mu(B_r)\le\pi r^2$. A tube of width $L$ and time length $L^2$, with one of the bounded velocities in the packet class, is covered by $O(L)$ space-time balls of radius $CL$. Its $\mu$-mass is therefore $O(L^3)$. A transverse enlargement by $2^\ell$ can be covered by $O(L2^{2\ell})$ such balls. Summing these bounds against the decay $2^{-M\ell}$, where $M$ is the fixed large exponent in <a href="#eq:src6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src6">[eq:src6]</a>, proves that condition with $D=1$ after a fixed enlargement of $m$. All constants here are uniform over $T$, the packet intercepts, and the center of $\mathcal B$.

We explain why the compact packet estimate applies to an exact unit-frequency extension $$Ev(X,T)=\int e^{i(X\cdot\eta-T|\eta|^2)}v(\eta)\,d\eta,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}v\subset\{c_0\le |\eta|\le C_0\}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:packets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:packets">3</a> at packet width $L$. It gives $v=\sum_{\omega,n}c_{\omega,n}v_{\omega,n}$, with $\omega\in L^{-1}\mathbb Z^2$, $n\in\mathbb Z^2$, and $$\sum_{\omega,n}|c_{\omega,n}|^2\le C\|v\|_2^2.$$ Its exact profile formula <a href="#eq:prelim-exact-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prelim-exact-profile">[eq:prelim-exact-profile]</a> reads $$\begin{gathered}
 Ev_{\omega,n}(X,T)
 =L^{-1}e^{i(X\cdot\omega-T|\omega|^2)}
 H\left(\frac{X-Ln-2\omega T}{L},\frac{T}{L^2}\right),
 \\[2pt]
 H(z,s)=\frac1{2\pi}\int\psi(\zeta)
                   e^{i(z\cdot\zeta-s|\zeta|^2)}\,d\zeta,
\end{gathered}$$ where $\psi$ is the fixed smooth frequency cutoff in that lemma. The phase-space counts are bounded, and, on every fixed bounded $s$-interval, all required derivatives of $H$ decrease faster than any fixed power of $|z|$. The remaining issue is its noncompact spatial support; we resolve it by summing compact layers.

To obtain the compact profiles of <a href="#eq:src5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src5">[eq:src5]</a>, choose a smooth time cutoff equal to one on $[0,1]$, and a spatial partition of unity $\sum_{q\in\mathbb Z^2}\rho(z-q)=1$ with $\rho$ compactly supported. Fix the finite profile derivative order required by Theorem <a href="#thm:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distribution">41</a>. In the $q$th term replace the intercept $Ln$ by $Ln+Lq$. The new profile is the old profile evaluated at $z+q$, multiplied by $\rho(z)$ and the time cutoff. For any prescribed large $A$, its required derivative bounds are $O((1+|q|)^{-A})$. Put this factor into the coefficients. For each fixed $q$, all phase-space intercepts undergo the same translation, so the counting bounds are preserved, and the new coefficient norm is at most $$C_A(1+|q|)^{-A}\|v\|_2.$$ Thus every layer is an admissible array for Theorem <a href="#thm:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distribution">41</a>.

<a id="eq:endpoint-exact-weak"></a>
The exponent $A$ is chosen once, after the required derivative order; the fixed profile bound may depend on both, but never on $L$ or $q$. For completeness, let $b_q>0$ satisfy $\sum_q b_q=1$ and $b_q\asymp(1+|q|)^{-3}$. Apply <a href="#eq:src12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src12">[eq:src12]</a> to the $q$th layer at threshold $b_q\lambda$, using homogeneity in its coefficients. Choosing $A$ sufficiently large makes $\sum_q b_q^{-p}(1+|q|)^{-Ap}$ finite and yields $$\mu\{|Ev|>\lambda\}
 \le C L^4\bigl(\lambda L^{4/3}\bigr)^{-p}\|v\|_2^p.
 \label{eq:endpoint-exact-weak}$$ These operations can first be made on finite packet sums. At a point, each frequency label has only $O(1)$ intercepts contributing to a fixed compact layer, and there are $O(L^2)$ frequency labels. Cauchy–Schwarz therefore bounds that layer by $C(1+|q|)^{-A}\|v\|_2$, uniformly in $L$; the factor $L$ from the square root of the label count cancels the packet amplitude $L^{-1}$. The same bound holds with the coefficient norm of any tail in place of $\|v\|_2$. Taking $A>2$ makes the layer sum absolutely and uniformly convergent, and square-summability makes finite coefficient truncations converge uniformly as well. Fourier-series partial sums also converge in $L^2$ on a fixed bounded frequency set, hence their exact extensions converge uniformly by Cauchy–Schwarz in frequency. These observations identify the compact layer sum with the exact extension. Passing to the limit in the distribution bound, first with a slightly smaller threshold, proves <a href="#eq:endpoint-exact-weak" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-exact-weak">[eq:endpoint-exact-weak]</a> for arbitrary $v\in L^2$ with the indicated support. This passage uses exact extensions and therefore introduces no time-dependent choices of representatives.

Now take $L=\sqrt N$ and $v(\eta)=N\widehat f_N(N\eta)$. Then $\|v\|_2=\|\widehat f_N\|_2$, and <a id="eq:endpoint-frequency-scaling"></a>$$S(t)f_N(x)=(2\pi)^{-2}N\,Ev(Nx,N^2t).
 \label{eq:endpoint-frequency-scaling}$$ A unit spatial ball becomes a ball of radius $N=L^2$, and a time interval of length $1/N$ becomes one of length $N=L^2$. For a measurable physical graph $t=t(x)$, its lifted spatial area in the new coordinates is $N^2$ times the original area. This cancels $L^4$ in <a href="#eq:endpoint-exact-weak" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-exact-weak">[eq:endpoint-exact-weak]</a>. The threshold scale in the physical field is $$N L^{-4/3}=N^{1/3}.$$ Consequently, for every such graph and every $\lambda>0$, $$\bigl|\{x\in B:|S(t(x))f_N(x)|>\lambda\}\bigr|
 \le C\left(\frac{N^{1/3}\|f_N\|_2}{\lambda}\right)^p.$$ For finitely many times choose, measurably, the first time attaining the largest modulus. Apply this graph bound, then increase the finite list to a countable dense set. Continuity of the bounded-frequency evolution identifies the resulting supremum with the supremum over all times and allows the endpoints. Finally, replacing $f_N$ by $S(t_0)f_N$ translates the interval without changing either its Fourier support or its $L^2$ norm. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Two dual estimates on a short time interval

<a id="eq:endpoint-dual-definitions"></a>
Fix a unit spatial ball $\Omega$, a measurable function $t:\Omega\to[0,1)$, and a measurable complex function $w$ with $|w|\le1$. These choices remain the same at every frequency. For $N=2^j$, $j\ge1$, let $\chi_N(\xi)=\chi(\xi/N)$ be a smooth cutoff supported on a fixed annulus away from zero. The same argument permits finitely many fixed choices of $\chi$. For a half-open time interval $I\subset[0,1)$, put $$\begin{aligned}
 E_I&=\{x\in\Omega:t(x)\in I\},
 &m_I&=|E_I|,\notag\\
 G_N(I)(\xi)&=\chi_N(\xi)\int_{E_I}
              w(x)e^{i(x\cdot\xi-t(x)|\xi|^2)}\,dx,
 &e_j(I)&=N^{-2/3}\|G_N(I)\|_2^2.
 \label{eq:endpoint-dual-definitions}
\end{aligned}$$ The quantities $m_I$ are the masses of a single measure on time, the pushforward of ${\bf1}_\Omega dx$ under $t$. This measure can have atoms; all interval partitions below are half-open so membership is unique.

<a id="eq:endpoint-weak-integration"></a>
We will use the elementary weak-space integration bound $$\int_F |h|\le \frac{p}{p-1}
             \|h\|_{L^{p,\infty}}|F|^{1-1/p}.
 \label{eq:endpoint-weak-integration}$$ Indeed the layer-cake formula bounds its left side by $\int_0^\infty\min\{|F|,(\|h\|_{L^{p,\infty}}/\lambda)^p\}\,d\lambda$; splitting where the two terms agree gives the stated expression.

<a id="eq:endpoint-kernel"></a>
The kernel for pairings of the dual functions is $$K_N(z,\tau)=\int |\chi_N(\xi)|^2
                   e^{i(z\cdot\xi-\tau|\xi|^2)}\,d\xi
 =N^2\int|\chi(\eta)|^2
                   e^{i(Nz\cdot\eta-N^2\tau|\eta|^2)}\,d\eta.
 \label{eq:endpoint-kernel}$$ In particular, $$\langle G_N(I),G_N(J)\rangle
 =\int_{E_I}\int_{E_J}w(x)\overline{w(y)}
       K_N(x-y,t(x)-t(y))\,dy\,dx.$$ Only the smooth frequency variable will be differentiated in estimates for this kernel. No regularity of the graph $t(x)$ is needed.

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoint-time-separation"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoint-time-separation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 48** (Separation in time). *Let $\mathcal D_j$ be the dyadic partition of $[0,1)$ into intervals of length $2^{-j}=1/N$. Then <a id="eq:endpoint-time-almost-orthogonality"></a>$$e_j([0,1))\le C\sum_{I\in\mathcal D_j}e_j(I)+CN^{-2}.
 \label{eq:endpoint-time-almost-orthogonality}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoint-time-separation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoint-time-separation</summary>

*Proof.* For two intervals whose indices differ by at most a sufficiently large fixed integer $C_1$, Hilbert-space Cauchy–Schwarz bounds their pairing by the geometric mean of their squared norms. Each interval has only $O(C_1)$ such neighbors, so their total contribution is at most $C\sum_I\|G_N(I)\|_2^2$.

For all other pairs, $|t(x)-t(y)|\ge(C_1-1)/N$, while $|x-y|\le2$. Write $z=x-y$ and $\tau=t(x)-t(y)$. On the support of $\chi$, the gradient $$\nabla_\eta(Nz\cdot\eta-N^2\tau|\eta|^2)
      =Nz-2N^2\tau\eta$$ has magnitude at least $cN^2|\tau|$ if $C_1$ is large enough, since the annulus has a positive inner radius. Dividing the phase by $N^2|\tau|$ gives a smooth phase with uniformly bounded derivatives and a uniformly nonvanishing gradient. Repeated integration by parts gives, for every fixed integer $M$, $$|K_N(z,\tau)|\le C_M N^2(N^2|\tau|)^{-M}
                 \le C_M N^{2-M}.$$ Integrate this bound once over the full set of far pairs, whose spatial measure is at most $|\Omega|^2$. There is no additional factor for the number of interval pairs. After normalization by $N^{-2/3}$, taking $M=4$ gives an error $O(N^{-8/3})$, which is bounded by $O(N^{-2})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoint-localized-dual"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoint-localized-dual" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:endpoint-mass-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:endpoint-spatial-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual"></a>
**Lemma 49** (Mass and spatial localization). *If $I\in\mathcal D_j$ and $J\subset I$ is a dyadic descendant at relative depth $k$, where $0\le k\le j$, set $r=2^{-k}$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 e_j(J)&\le C m_J^{2-2/p},
 \label{eq:endpoint-mass-bound}\\
 e_j(J)&\le C r^{2/3}m_J.
 \label{eq:endpoint-spatial-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently, with $$\alpha=\frac32-\frac1p=1+\frac{1-2/p}{2}>1,$$ we have $$e_j(J)\le C2^{-k/3}m_J^\alpha.
 \label{eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoint-localized-dual"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoint-localized-dual</summary>

*Proof.* For the first estimate, dualize the frequency $L^2$ norm in <a href="#eq:endpoint-dual-definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-dual-definitions">[eq:endpoint-dual-definitions]</a>. Against a frequency test function of norm one, the spatial integrand is an annular Schrödinger extension with input norm bounded by a fixed constant. Its values at $t(x)$ are bounded by its short-time maximal function on the interval $J$, whose length is at most $1/N$. Proposition <a href="#prop:shortmax" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shortmax">47</a> and <a href="#eq:endpoint-weak-integration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-weak-integration">[eq:endpoint-weak-integration]</a> therefore give $$\|G_N(J)\|_2\le C N^{1/3}m_J^{1-1/p}.$$ Squaring and normalizing proves <a href="#eq:endpoint-mass-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-mass-bound">[eq:endpoint-mass-bound]</a>.

For the second estimate, partition space into half-open squares $Q$ of side $r$, and write $G_N(J,Q)$ for the same dual integral over $E_J\cap Q$. Set $m_{J,Q}=|E_J\cap Q|$. Declare two squares neighbors if their grid indices differ by at most a sufficiently large fixed constant $C_2$. Neighbor interactions are bounded by $C\sum_Q\|G_N(J,Q)\|_2^2$, using Cauchy–Schwarz and the bounded number of neighbors of each square.

<a id="eq:endpoint-spatial-far-error"></a>
For non-neighboring squares, $|x-y|\ge cC_2r$, whereas $|t(x)-t(y)|\le |J|=r/N$. Choose $C_2$ using the outer radius of the annular support. The spatial part now dominates the gradient of the phase in <a href="#eq:endpoint-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-kernel">[eq:endpoint-kernel]</a>, and integration by parts gives $$|K_N(x-y,t(x)-t(y))|
       \le C N^2(1+N|x-y|)^{-4}.$$ The right side has a uniformly bounded spatial integral. Since the graph measure is projected spatial area and $|w|\le1$, the total normalized contribution of these far pairs is at most $$C N^{-2/3}\int_{E_J}\int_{\mathbb R^2}
           N^2(1+N|x-y|)^{-4}\,dy\,dx
 \le C N^{-2/3}m_J\le C r^{2/3}m_J.
 \label{eq:endpoint-spatial-far-error}$$ The last inequality is exactly where $k\le j$, equivalently $Nr\ge1$, is used.

It remains to estimate a single square contribution. Let $x_Q$ be the center of $Q$ and $t_J$ the left endpoint of $J$. Make the change $$x=x_Q+rX,\qquad t=t_J+r^2s,\qquad \xi=\eta/r.$$ The new graph lies in a unit spatial ball, has spatial area $r^{-2}m_{J,Q}$, and has times in an interval of length $$\frac{|J|}{r^2}=\frac1{Nr}.$$ The new annular frequency is $Nr\ge1$. The dual function acquires the factor $r^2$ from $dx$ and a unimodular factor from $x_Q,t_J$; changing frequency measure gives $$\|G_N(J,Q)\|_2^2=r^2\|\widetilde G_{Nr}\|_2^2.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:shortmax" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shortmax">47</a> and <a href="#eq:endpoint-weak-integration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-weak-integration">[eq:endpoint-weak-integration]</a> directly at the normalized scale, including its lowest possible value $Nr=1$. This gives $$\begin{aligned}
 N^{-2/3}\|G_N(J,Q)\|_2^2
 &\le C N^{-2/3}r^2(Nr)^{2/3}
                     (r^{-2}m_{J,Q})^{2-2/p}\\
 &=C r^{-4/3+4/p}m_{J,Q}^{2-2/p}
 \le C r^{2/3}m_{J,Q},
\end{aligned}$$ where the last step uses $m_{J,Q}\le r^2$ and $2-2/p>1$. Sum this estimate over $Q$, include the bounded neighbor interactions and <a href="#eq:endpoint-spatial-far-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-spatial-far-error">[eq:endpoint-spatial-far-error]</a>, and obtain <a href="#eq:endpoint-spatial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-spatial-bound">[eq:endpoint-spatial-bound]</a>. Finally, the geometric mean of <a href="#eq:endpoint-mass-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-mass-bound">[eq:endpoint-mass-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:endpoint-spatial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-spatial-bound">[eq:endpoint-spatial-bound]</a> is <a href="#eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual">[eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
#### The common time tree and the Sobolev square norm

The next step uses the same mass assignment $I\mapsto m_I$ for every $j$. Choosing a different graph separately at each frequency would destroy the telescoping argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoint-square-sum"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoint-square-sum" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:endpoint-square-sum"></a>
**Lemma 50** (Summation on the common time tree). *For every graph and phase choice in <a href="#eq:endpoint-dual-definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-dual-definitions">[eq:endpoint-dual-definitions]</a>, $$\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-2j/3}\|G_{2^j}([0,1))\|_2^2\le C.
 \label{eq:endpoint-square-sum}$$ The constant is independent of $t$, $w$, and the center of $\Omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoint-square-sum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoint-square-sum</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $j\ge1$ and $I\in\mathcal D_j$. Starting from $H_{I,0}=I$, select at each step a child with larger mass, breaking ties consistently by choosing the left child. Call the selected child $H_{I,k}$ and the other child $P_{I,k}$, for $1\le k\le j$. Thus $$m_{P_{I,k}}\le m_{H_{I,k}},\qquad
 I=\left(\mathop{\dot\bigcup}_{k=1}^jP_{I,k}\right)
                    \mathbin{\dot\cup}H_{I,j}.$$ The second identity is an exact partition of time, including any atoms of the time measure. Figure <a href="#fig:endpoint-tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:endpoint-tree">3</a> records this partition and the two different depths used in the summation.

<figure id="fig:endpoint-tree">

<figcaption>Three steps of the heavier-child path. At each step the lighter sibling is set aside; the chosen child is split again. The dropped siblings together with the terminal child partition $I$. For fixed relative depth $k$, changing the frequency index $j$ changes the absolute depth of the parent. This makes the parents distinct in the sum controlled by the convexity deficit.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-endpoint-tree"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:endpoint-tree`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  interval/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,inner sep=5pt,
                   fill=blue!4,font=\small},
  lighter/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,inner sep=5pt,
                  fill=orange!12,font=\small},
  heavyedge/.style={line width=1.1pt,blue!65!black},
  lightedge/.style={line width=.6pt,orange!65!black},
  note/.style={font=\footnotesize,align=left}
]
\node[interval] (root) at (0,0) {$I=H_{I,0}$};
\node[lighter] (p1) at (-1.8,-1.2) {$P_{I,1}$};
\node[interval] (h1) at (1.0,-1.2) {$H_{I,1}$};
\node[lighter] (p2) at (-.8,-2.4) {$P_{I,2}$};
\node[interval] (h2) at (2.0,-2.4) {$H_{I,2}$};
\node[lighter] (p3) at (.2,-3.6) {$P_{I,3}$};
\node[interval] (h3) at (3.0,-3.6) {$H_{I,3}$};
\draw[lightedge] (root)--(p1);
\draw[heavyedge] (root)--node[right,note] {larger mass}(h1);
\draw[lightedge] (h1)--(p2);
\draw[heavyedge] (h1)--(h2);
\draw[lightedge] (h2)--(p3);
\draw[heavyedge] (h2)--(h3);
\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,0) {absolute depth $j$};
\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,-1.2) {relative depth $k=1$};
\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,-2.4) {$k=2$};
\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,-3.6) {$k=3$};
\node[note,anchor=west] at (3.6,-1.0)
  {Dropped pieces are disjoint.\\
   Each is a lighter child.};
\node[note,anchor=west] at (3.6,-2.5)
  {Parent of $P_{I,k}$:\\
   absolute depth $j+k-1$.};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 77 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A dyadic tree branching structure depicting three steps of a heavier-child path:
- Root box at the top labeled "$I = H_{I,0}$", labeled on the left with "absolute depth $j$".
- First branch level ("relative depth $k = 1$"): Left child is in a box labeled "$P_{I,1}$"; right child (with edge labeled "larger mass") is in a box labeled "$H_{I,1}$".
- Second branch level ("$k = 2$"): Branching from "$H_{I,1}$", left child is in a box labeled "$P_{I,2}$"; right child is in a box labeled "$H_{I,2}$".
- Third branch level ("$k = 3$"): Branching from "$H_{I,2}$", left child is in a box labeled "$P_{I,3}$"; right child is in a box labeled "$H_{I,3}$".
- Annotations on the right side:
  - Text block: "Dropped pieces are disjoint. Each is a lighter child."
  - Text block: "Parent of $P_{I,k}$: absolute depth $j + k - 1$."
```


<a id="eq:endpoint-heavy-path"></a>
By additivity of $G_N$ and the triangle inequality in frequency $L^2$, followed by <a href="#eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual">[eq:endpoint-interpolated-dual]</a>, $$e_j(I)^{1/2}
 \le C\sum_{k=1}^j2^{-k/6}m_{P_{I,k}}^{\alpha/2}
          +C2^{-j/6}m_{H_{I,j}}^{\alpha/2}.$$ Use Cauchy–Schwarz with weights $2^{-k/6}$, including one additional terminal weight $2^{-j/6}$. Their sum is bounded independently of $j$. Since $m_{H_{I,j}}\le m_I$, we obtain $$e_j(I)\le C\sum_{k=1}^j2^{-k/6}m_{P_{I,k}}^\alpha
                    +C2^{-j/6}m_I^\alpha.
 \label{eq:endpoint-heavy-path}$$

<a id="eq:endpoint-convexity-deficit"></a>
We give the convexity calculation behind the sum of the first terms. If a parent has child masses $a\ge b\ge0$, then $$(a+b)^\alpha-a^\alpha-b^\alpha
       \ge(2^\alpha-2)b^\alpha.
 \label{eq:endpoint-convexity-deficit}$$ For $b>0$, divide by $b^\alpha$ and put $u=a/b\ge1$. The function $(u+1)^\alpha-u^\alpha-1$ is increasing for $u\ge1$, so its minimum is $2^\alpha-2$; the case $b=0$ is immediate. Summing parent-minus-children deficits through depth $d-1$ telescopes to $$m_{[0,1)}^\alpha-\sum_{Q\in\mathcal D_d}m_Q^\alpha
       \le m_{[0,1)}^\alpha.$$ The deficits are nonnegative. Letting $d\to\infty$ shows that the sum of the lighter-child $\alpha$-powers over any collection of distinct dyadic parents is at most $m_{[0,1)}^\alpha/(2^\alpha-2)$.

Now fix $k\ge1$ and range over all $j\ge k$ and all $I\in\mathcal D_j$. The parent of $P_{I,k}$ is $H_{I,k-1}$, at absolute depth $j+k-1$. Different $j$ give different absolute depths; for the same $j$, different $I$ have disjoint descendants. These parents are therefore distinct. Consequently, $$\sum_{j\ge k}\sum_{I\in\mathcal D_j}m_{P_{I,k}}^\alpha
      \le \frac{m_{[0,1)}^\alpha}{2^\alpha-2}.$$ For the terminal terms, superadditivity of the $\alpha$th power gives $\sum_{I\in\mathcal D_j}m_I^\alpha\le m_{[0,1)}^\alpha$. Thus summing <a href="#eq:endpoint-heavy-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-heavy-path">[eq:endpoint-heavy-path]</a>, first at fixed $k$ and then in $k$, gives $$\sum_{j\ge1}\sum_{I\in\mathcal D_j}e_j(I)
 \le C m_{[0,1)}^\alpha
       \left(\sum_{k\ge1}2^{-k/6}+\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j/6}\right)
 \le C.$$ All sums are of nonnegative terms, so these rearrangements also follow from finite truncations. Finally apply Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoint-time-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoint-time-separation">48</a> and sum its $O(2^{-2j})$ errors. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-2"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 51*. The strict inequality $p>2$ is used quantitatively here: $\alpha=3/2-1/p>1$ and $2^\alpha-2>0$. At $p=2$ the convexity deficit in <a href="#eq:endpoint-convexity-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-convexity-deficit">[eq:endpoint-convexity-deficit]</a> vanishes. The gain in the time tree therefore cannot be recovered merely from a weak $L^2$ version of <a href="#eq:src24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src24">[eq:src24]</a>.

</div>

<a id="stmt-thm-maximal"></a>
<div id="thm:maximal" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:endpoint-strong-maximal"></a>
**Theorem 52** (Local strong maximal estimate). *There is a constant $C$ such that, for every $f\in H^{1/3}(\mathbb R^2)$ whose Fourier transform has compact support and every $x_0\in\mathbb R^2$, $$\int_{B(x_0,1)}\sup_{0\le t<1}|S(t)f(x)|\,dx
       \le C\|f\|_{H^{1/3}}.
 \label{eq:endpoint-strong-maximal}$$ For arbitrary $f\in H^{1/3}(\mathbb R^2)$ there is a jointly measurable representative $v_f(x,t)$, $0\le t<1$, continuous in $t$ for almost every $x$, equal to $S(t)f$ as an $L^2$ function for each fixed $t$, and satisfying the same bound with $S(t)f(x)$ replaced by $v_f(x,t)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-maximal"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-maximal</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose $\widehat f$ has compact support. Take a smooth frequency partition $$\psi_0+\sum_{j\ge1}\psi_j=1,$$ where $\psi_0$ is supported in a fixed ball and $\psi_j$ is supported on an annulus $|\xi|\asymp2^j$, with uniformly bounded overlap. Put $\widehat f_j=\psi_j\widehat f$. Choose enlarged smooth annular cutoffs $\chi_{2^j}$ equal to one on $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi_j$. These are the cutoffs used in Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoint-square-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoint-square-sum">50</a>.

For any common graph $t(x)$ and phase $w(x)$, Fourier inversion and the definition of $G_N$ give $$\int_\Omega w(x)\sum_{j\ge1}S(t(x))f_j(x)\,dx
       =(2\pi)^{-2}\sum_{j\ge1}
             \int\widehat f_j(\xi)G_{2^j}([0,1))(\xi)\,d\xi.$$ Only finitely many summands are nonzero. Cauchy–Schwarz in frequency and in $j$ bounds the modulus by <a id="eq:endpoint-frequency-cauchy-schwarz"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 C\left(\sum_{j\ge1}2^{2j/3}\|\widehat f_j\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}
  \left(\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-2j/3}
              \|G_{2^j}([0,1))\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}
 &\le C\|f\|_{H^{1/3}}.
 \label{eq:endpoint-frequency-cauchy-schwarz}
\end{aligned}$$ The first factor is bounded by the Sobolev square norm because on the support of $\psi_j$ one has $2^{2j/3}\asymp(1+|\xi|^2)^{1/3}$, and the supports overlap boundedly. The second factor is bounded by <a href="#eq:endpoint-square-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-square-sum">[eq:endpoint-square-sum]</a>. The low-frequency term satisfies $\sup_{x,t}|S(t)f_0(x)|\le C\|f_0\|_2$ by Cauchy–Schwarz on its fixed frequency ball, so its integral over $\Omega$ has the same bound.

To recover the maximal function, take any finite list of times in $[0,1)$ and let $t(x)$ be the first time attaining the maximal modulus of $S(t)f(x)$ on that list. Set $w(x)=\overline{S(t(x))f(x)}/|S(t(x))f(x)|$ where the denominator is nonzero, and set $w(x)=0$ otherwise. These are measurable choices, and the linearized integral is exactly the integral of the finite maximum. Increase the list to a countable dense subset of $[0,1)$ containing zero. Continuity in time and monotone convergence prove <a href="#eq:endpoint-strong-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-strong-maximal">[eq:endpoint-strong-maximal]</a>. The estimates above used only fixed ball radii and spatial differences, so the constant is uniform in $x_0$.

For general $f$, choose $f_n$ with $\widehat f_n\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ and $\|f_n-f\|_{H^{1/3}}\le2^{-n}$. In particular, $$\sum_{n\ge1}\|f_{n+1}-f_n\|_{H^{1/3}}<\infty.$$ Define the measurable functions $$d_n(x)=\sup_{0\le t<1}
              |S(t)f_{n+1}(x)-S(t)f_n(x)|.$$ The supremum can be taken over rational times, together with zero. By <a href="#eq:endpoint-strong-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-strong-maximal">[eq:endpoint-strong-maximal]</a>, $$\sup_{x_0\in\mathbb R^2}\int_{B(x_0,1)}\sum_{n\ge1}d_n(x)\,dx
 \le C\sum_{n\ge1}\|f_{n+1}-f_n\|_{H^{1/3}}<\infty.$$ A countable covering of $\mathbb R^2$ by unit balls therefore gives a single full-measure set $E_0$ on which $\sum_n d_n(x)<\infty$. For each $x\in E_0$ the sequence $S(t)f_n(x)$ converges uniformly over $0\le t<1$. Denote its limit by $v_f(x,t)$, and define $v_f(x,t)=0$ for $x\notin E_0$. It is jointly measurable, and its time dependence is continuous at every point of $E_0$, as a uniform limit of continuous functions.

For each fixed $t$, unitarity gives $S(t)f_n\to S(t)f$ in $L^2$. The pointwise limit on $E_0$ must therefore agree almost everywhere with that $L^2$ limit. This identifies the $L^2$ class of $v_f(\cdot,t)$ for every fixed $t$; it does not assert a pointwise intersection over uncountably many times. Finally, Fatou’s lemma in the maximal estimate gives $$\int_{B(x_0,1)}\sup_{0\le t<1}|v_f(x,t)|\,dx
    \le C\lim_{n\to\infty}\|f_n\|_{H^{1/3}}
    =C\|f\|_{H^{1/3}}.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Recovering the Gaussian-first, all-real-time limit

<a id="eq:endpoint-envelope-local-mass"></a>
Fix $f\in H^{1/3}(\mathbb R^2)$ and the approximants just constructed, and write $v=v_f$. The same argument gives error envelopes with both pointwise decay and spatial control at infinity. Namely, let <a id="eq:endpoint-error-envelope"></a>$$M_n(x)=\sup_{0\le t<1}|v(x,t)-S(t)f_n(x)|.
 \label{eq:endpoint-error-envelope}$$ These functions are measurable by the rational-time supremum, since the chosen $v(x,\cdot)$ is continuous even on the complement where it was defined to be zero. On $E_0$ the convergence is uniform, so $M_n(x)\to0$. Applying <a href="#eq:endpoint-strong-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-strong-maximal">[eq:endpoint-strong-maximal]</a> to $f_\ell-f_n$ and then using Fatou yields $$\sup_{x_0\in\mathbb R^2}\int_{B(x_0,1)}M_n(x)\,dx
       \le C\|f-f_n\|_{H^{1/3}}.
 \label{eq:endpoint-envelope-local-mass}$$ In particular, every $M_n$ is locally integrable with uniformly bounded integrals over all translated unit balls. This uniformity controls Gaussian convolution far from its evaluation point.

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoint-heat-envelope"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoint-heat-envelope" class="lemma">

**Lemma 53** (Gaussian approximation with uniform local mass). *Let $h\ge0$ be locally integrable on $\mathbb R^2$ and suppose $$A=\sup_{z\in\mathbb R^2}\int_{B(z,1)}h(y)\,dy<\infty.$$ For $a>0$ let $$K_a(z)=\frac1{4\pi a}e^{-|z|^2/(4a)}.$$ Then $(K_a*h)(x)$ is finite for every $a>0$ and every $x$. At every Lebesgue point $x$ of $h$, <a id="eq:endpoint-heat-envelope-limit"></a>$$\lim_{a\downarrow0}(K_a*h)(x)=h(x).
 \label{eq:endpoint-heat-envelope-limit}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoint-heat-envelope"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoint-heat-envelope</summary>

<a id="eq:endpoint-gaussian-tail"></a>
*Proof.* For a fixed $\rho>0$, cover each annulus $2^\ell\rho\le |z|<2^{\ell+1}\rho$ by at most $C(1+2^\ell\rho)^2$ unit balls. Positivity gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{|z|\ge\rho}K_a(z)h(x-z)\,dz
 &\le \frac{CA}{a}\sum_{\ell\ge0}(1+2^\ell\rho)^2
                       e^{-4^\ell\rho^2/(4a)}\notag\\
 &\le C_\rho A a^{-1}e^{-\rho^2/(8a)},
 \qquad 0<a\le\min\{1,\rho^2\}.
 \label{eq:endpoint-gaussian-tail}
\end{aligned}$$ The series in the first line is finite for every fixed $a>0$. Together with local integrability this proves finiteness of the convolution, and the second line shows that its distant contribution tends to zero.

At a Lebesgue point $x$, put $H_x(r)=\int_{|z|<r}|h(x-z)-h(x)|\,dz=o(r^2)$. Given $\varepsilon>0$, choose $\rho>0$ so that $H_x(r)\le\varepsilon r^2$ for $0<r\le\rho$. With $k_a(r)=(4\pi a)^{-1}e^{-r^2/(4a)}$, radial integration by parts gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{|z|<\rho}K_a(z)|h(x-z)-h(x)|\,dz
 &=k_a(\rho)H_x(\rho)
       +\int_0^\rho(-k_a'(r))H_x(r)\,dr\\
 &\le\varepsilon\left(k_a(\rho)\rho^2
                  +\int_0^\rho(-k_a'(r))r^2\,dr\right)
 \le C\varepsilon.
\end{aligned}$$ Outside this neighborhood, <a href="#eq:endpoint-gaussian-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-gaussian-tail">[eq:endpoint-gaussian-tail]</a> controls the $h$ term, and the constant $h(x)$ is controlled by the vanishing Gaussian mass outside $B(0,\rho)$. Let $a\downarrow0$ and then $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoint-heat-envelope-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoint-heat-envelope</summary>

<a id="eq:endpoint-common-good-set"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* At time zero, the fixed-time $L^2$ identification in Theorem <a href="#thm:maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:maximal">52</a> gives $v(\cdot,0)=f$ in $L^2$. Since $f^*$ represents the same $L^2$ function on the set of Lebesgue points of $f$, intersect $E_0$ with the full-measure set where $v(x,0)=f^*(x)$ and with that Lebesgue-point set. Next intersect with the Lebesgue-point sets of the countably many functions $M_n$. Call the resulting full-measure set $E$. For every $x\in E$ we now have, simultaneously, $$\begin{gathered}
 v(x,\cdot)\text{ is continuous on }[0,1),\qquad v(x,0)=f^*(x),\\
 M_n(x)\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 (K_a*M_n)(x)\longrightarrow M_n(x)
       \quad\text{as }a\downarrow0\text{ for every }n.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:endpoint-common-good-set}$$ The last assertion follows from <a href="#eq:endpoint-envelope-local-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-envelope-local-mass">[eq:endpoint-envelope-local-mass]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoint-heat-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoint-heat-envelope">53</a>. Only countably many exceptional sets were removed.

<a id="eq:endpoint-exact-convolution"></a>
For each fixed $t\in[0,1)$, the function $v(\cdot,t)$ represents $S(t)f$ in $L^2$. Since $K_a(x-\cdot)\in L^2$, convolution at a given $x$ is an $L^2$ pairing and is independent of the representative of this $L^2$ class. Plancherel’s identity therefore gives $$(K_a*v(\cdot,t))(x)
  =\frac1{(2\pi)^2}\int e^{ix\cdot\xi-it|\xi|^2-a|\xi|^2}
                                \widehat f(\xi)\,d\xi
  =U_af(x,t)
 \label{eq:endpoint-exact-convolution}$$ for every $x\in\mathbb R^2$, every $a>0$, and every $0<t<1$. The integral is absolutely convergent by Cauchy–Schwarz. The quantifiers in this step are significant: for each arbitrary $t$, an identity of $L^2$ classes gives the convolution identity at every evaluation point. There is no intersection of time-dependent pointwise exceptional sets in <a href="#eq:endpoint-exact-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-exact-convolution">[eq:endpoint-exact-convolution]</a>.

<a id="eq:endpoint-smooth-heat-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:endpoint-uniform-gaussian-comparison"></a>
For the approximants, absolute Fourier integrability yields $$\begin{aligned}
 R_n(a)&:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb R^2,\,0<t<1}
               |U_af_n(x,t)-S(t)f_n(x)|\notag\\
 &\le\frac1{(2\pi)^2}\int
               |1-e^{-a|\xi|^2}|\,|\widehat f_n(\xi)|\,d\xi
       \longrightarrow0\qquad(a\downarrow0).
 \label{eq:endpoint-smooth-heat-limit}
\end{aligned}$$ Positivity of $K_a$, the error envelope, and <a href="#eq:endpoint-exact-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-exact-convolution">[eq:endpoint-exact-convolution]</a> give, for each $x\in E$, $$\sup_{0<t<1}|U_af(x,t)-v(x,t)|
 \le (K_a*M_n)(x)+M_n(x)+R_n(a).
 \label{eq:endpoint-uniform-gaussian-comparison}$$ Indeed the difference between the two convolutions is bounded by $K_a*M_n$, because $|v(y,t)-S(t)f_n(y)|\le M_n(y)$ for almost every $y$, simultaneously for all $t$; the remaining two differences are bounded by $R_n(a)$ and $M_n(x)$. All these convolutions are finite by the preceding lemma or by the $L^2$ pairing.

Fix $n$ and take $\limsup_{a\downarrow0}$ in <a href="#eq:endpoint-uniform-gaussian-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-uniform-gaussian-comparison">[eq:endpoint-uniform-gaussian-comparison]</a>. By <a href="#eq:endpoint-common-good-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-common-good-set">[eq:endpoint-common-good-set]</a> and <a href="#eq:endpoint-smooth-heat-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-smooth-heat-limit">[eq:endpoint-smooth-heat-limit]</a>, $$\limsup_{a\downarrow0}\sup_{0<t<1}|U_af(x,t)-v(x,t)|
        \le2M_n(x).$$ Let $n\to\infty$. Thus for every $x\in E$, <a id="eq:endpoint-uniform-gaussian-limit"></a>$$\lim_{a\downarrow0}\sup_{0<t<1}|U_af(x,t)-v(x,t)|=0.
 \label{eq:endpoint-uniform-gaussian-limit}$$ In particular, at each such $x$ the finite limit defining $u_f(x,t)$ exists for every real $0<t<1$, with $t$ fixed before $a$ tends to zero, and it equals $v(x,t)$. Continuity at zero now gives $$\lim_{\substack{t\downarrow0\\t\in\mathbb R}}u_f(x,t)
     =\lim_{t\downarrow0}v(x,t)=v(x,0)=f^*(x).$$ This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Dependencies of the proof. Small scale losses are allowed in the transverse and sparse arguments; the frame and dyadic distribution estimates must be uniform in the packet scale.",
      "figure_id": "fig:dependencies",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:dependencies",
          "exact_quote": "Dependencies of the proof. Small scale losses are allowed in the\ntransverse and sparse arguments; the frame and dyadic distribution estimates\nmust be uniform in the packet scale.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n node distance=9mm and 13mm,\n box/.style={draw=black!65,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,\n             text width=39mm,minimum height=10mm,font=\\small,inner sep=4pt},\n arrow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},semithick}]\n \\node[box] (trans) {Transverse power saving};\n \\node[box,below=of trans] (sparse) {Sparse packet estimate};\n \\node[box,right=of trans] (frame) {Uniform bilinear frame estimate};\n \\node[box,below=of frame] (flat) {Flat-frequency estimates};\n \\node[box,right=of flat] (geom) {Rich-tube geometry};\n \\node[box,below=14mm of flat] (dist) {Uniform dyadic\\\\distribution bound};\n \\node[box,below=of dist] (sum) {Endpoint square summation};\n \\node[box,below=of sum] (limit) {Gaussian limit at all times};\n \\draw[arrow] (trans)--(sparse);\n \\draw[arrow] (frame)--(flat);\n \\draw[arrow] (frame.east) -- ++(6mm,0) |- ([yshift=2.5mm]dist.east);\n \\draw[arrow] (sparse.south)|-(dist.west);\n \\draw[arrow] (flat)--(dist);\n \\draw[arrow] (geom.south)|-(dist.east);\n \\draw[arrow] (dist)--(sum);\n \\draw[arrow] (sum)--(limit);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart of proof dependencies with rectangular boxes arranged in five vertical tiers:\n- Top tier: \"Transverse power saving\" (left) and \"Uniform bilinear frame estimate\" (right).\n- Second tier: \"Sparse packet estimate\" (left, pointed to by a downward arrow from \"Transverse power saving\"), \"Flat-frequency estimates\" (middle, pointed to by a downward arrow from \"Uniform bilinear frame estimate\"), and \"Rich-tube geometry\" (right, pointed to by a downward arrow from \"Uniform bilinear frame estimate\").\n- Third tier: \"Uniform dyadic distribution bound\" (center), receiving incoming arrows from \"Sparse packet estimate\", \"Flat-frequency estimates\", and \"Rich-tube geometry\".\n- Fourth tier: \"Endpoint square summation\" (center), receiving a downward arrow from \"Uniform dyadic distribution bound\".\n- Bottom tier: \"Gaussian limit at all times\" (center, standalone box below \"Endpoint square summation\")."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Geometry in the bin plane $P_{π}$ (schematic projection). The shaded horizontal band is the near region removed in [eq:geometry-near-mass]; the thin sloping strip contains the sampled tube after enlargement. Since its direction is separated from the target, only a controlled length lies in the near band. The true plate also has thickness $w$ perpendicular to this picture.",
      "figure_id": "fig:geometry-far",
      "pdf_page": 58,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:geometry-far",
          "exact_quote": "Geometry in the bin plane $P_\\pi$ (schematic projection).\nThe shaded horizontal band is the near region removed in\n\\eqref{eq:geometry-near-mass}; the thin sloping strip contains the\nsampled tube after enlargement. Since its direction is sep",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9,>=Stealth,font=\\small]\n \\fill[black!4] (-0.5,-0.7) rectangle (6.2,0.7);\n \\draw[densely dashed,black!45] (-0.5,0.7)--(6.2,0.7);\n \\draw[densely dashed,black!45] (-0.5,-0.7)--(6.2,-0.7);\n \\draw[->] (-0.5,0)--(6.5,0) node[right] {$e$};\n \\draw[->] (0,-1.25)--(0,2.85) node[above] {$u_\\pi$};\n \\node[below] at (5.15,0) {target line $\\ell$};\n \\fill[blue!9] (0.5,-1.1)--(0.95,-1.1)--(4.7,2.5)--(4.25,2.5)--cycle;\n \\draw[blue!65!black,thick,->] (0.73,-1.1)--(4.48,2.5)\n       node[above right] {sampled direction $e'$};\n \\draw[blue!50!black,densely dashed] (0.5,-1.1)--(4.25,2.5);\n \\draw[blue!50!black,densely dashed] (0.95,-1.1)--(4.7,2.5);\n \\fill (1.88,0) circle (1.5pt);\n \\node[below right] at (1.88,0) {$Hs$};\n \\fill[blue!65!black] (3.55,1.61) circle (2pt);\n \\node[right] at (3.67,1.5) {retained far mass};\n \\draw[<->,black!65] (5.8,0.04)--(5.8,0.68)\n       node[midway,right] {$R_{\\mathrm f}$};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (0.15,2.65) {$P_\\pi$};\n \\node[anchor=west,blue!65!black] at (0.4,-1.6)\n       {strip: $|x-Hs-ay|\\leq w$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "2D geometric schematic projection in the bin plane $P_\\pi$:\n- A vertical axis on the left labeled $u_\\pi$ with an upward arrow, with a point marked $P_\\pi$.\n- A horizontal line labeled \"target line $\\ell$\" with an arrow pointing right labeled $e$.\n- A horizontal shaded band surrounding the target line $\\ell$, with its vertical half-height marked by a vertical double-headed arrow labeled $R_f$.\n- A thin sloping strip crossing the horizontal band at an angle, labeled below as \"strip: $|x - Hs - ay| \\le w$\".\n- An arrow along the upper portion of the strip pointing in the direction of the strip labeled \"sampled direction $e'$\".\n- The portion of the strip inside the shaded band is labeled $Hs$.\n- The portion of the sloping strip above the near band contains hatching and is labeled with an arrow pointing to \"retained far mass\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Three steps of the heavier-child path. At each step the lighter sibling is set aside; the chosen child is split again. The dropped siblings together with the terminal child partition $I$. For fixed relative depth $k$, changing the frequency index $j$ changes the absolute depth of the parent. This makes the parents distinct in the sum controlled by the convexity deficit.",
      "figure_id": "fig:endpoint-tree",
      "pdf_page": 77,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:endpoint-tree",
          "exact_quote": "Three steps of the heavier-child path.  At each step the\n lighter sibling is set aside; the chosen child is split again.  The\n dropped siblings together with the terminal child partition $I$.\n For fixed relative depth $k$, changing the freq",
          "source_object": "fig:0003"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  interval/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,inner sep=5pt,\n                   fill=blue!4,font=\\small},\n  lighter/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,inner sep=5pt,\n                  fill=orange!12,font=\\small},\n  heavyedge/.style={line width=1.1pt,blue!65!black},\n  lightedge/.style={line width=.6pt,orange!65!black},\n  note/.style={font=\\footnotesize,align=left}\n]\n\\node[interval] (root) at (0,0) {$I=H_{I,0}$};\n\\node[lighter] (p1) at (-1.8,-1.2) {$P_{I,1}$};\n\\node[interval] (h1) at (1.0,-1.2) {$H_{I,1}$};\n\\node[lighter] (p2) at (-.8,-2.4) {$P_{I,2}$};\n\\node[interval] (h2) at (2.0,-2.4) {$H_{I,2}$};\n\\node[lighter] (p3) at (.2,-3.6) {$P_{I,3}$};\n\\node[interval] (h3) at (3.0,-3.6) {$H_{I,3}$};\n\\draw[lightedge] (root)--(p1);\n\\draw[heavyedge] (root)--node[right,note] {larger mass}(h1);\n\\draw[lightedge] (h1)--(p2);\n\\draw[heavyedge] (h1)--(h2);\n\\draw[lightedge] (h2)--(p3);\n\\draw[heavyedge] (h2)--(h3);\n\\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,0) {absolute depth $j$};\n\\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,-1.2) {relative depth $k=1$};\n\\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,-2.4) {$k=2$};\n\\node[note,anchor=east] at (-2.6,-3.6) {$k=3$};\n\\node[note,anchor=west] at (3.6,-1.0)\n  {Dropped pieces are disjoint.\\\\\n   Each is a lighter child.};\n\\node[note,anchor=west] at (3.6,-2.5)\n  {Parent of $P_{I,k}$:\\\\\n   absolute depth $j+k-1$.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A dyadic tree branching structure depicting three steps of a heavier-child path:\n- Root box at the top labeled \"$I = H_{I,0}$\", labeled on the left with \"absolute depth $j$\".\n- First branch level (\"relative depth $k = 1$\"): Left child is in a box labeled \"$P_{I,1}$\"; right child (with edge labeled \"larger mass\") is in a box labeled \"$H_{I,1}$\".\n- Second branch level (\"$k = 2$\"): Branching from \"$H_{I,1}$\", left child is in a box labeled \"$P_{I,2}$\"; right child is in a box labeled \"$H_{I,2}$\".\n- Third branch level (\"$k = 3$\"): Branching from \"$H_{I,2}$\", left child is in a box labeled \"$P_{I,3}$\"; right child is in a box labeled \"$H_{I,3}$\".\n- Annotations on the right side:\n  - Text block: \"Dropped pieces are disjoint. Each is a lighter child.\"\n  - Text block: \"Parent of $P_{I,k}$: absolute depth $j + k - 1$.\""
    }
  ],
